Cyber Insurers See New Divide: AI Defense 'Haves' &amp; 'Have Nots'

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Dark Reading Confidential Episode 23: Cyber insurers share their view of the AI-driven threat landscape and how mounting challenges are putting pressure on both sides of the policy, leaving the current model unsustainable.

Cyber insurance providers are approaching 2027 facing a fundamental challenge. Claims rose 40% in 2024 and another 20% in 2025, even as premiums have stayed largely flat. This trend is something both Coalition and Travelers say isn't sustainable; and at the center of that shift is AI, which is reshaping the threat landscape not by inventing new attack types, but by making existing ones — ransomware, business email compromise, and phishing — dramatically easier to execute at scale.

In this episode of Dark Reading Confidential, Dark Reading media and projects director Becky Bracken and Dark Reading senior news director Rob Wright sit down with Lauren Winchester, vice president of cyber-risk services at Travelers; and Daniel Woods, head of emerging risk at Coalition, to unpack what the data actually shows. The conversation moves past the headline-grabbing surge in disclosed vulnerabilities to a more nuanced picture: exploitation is rising, but not as sharply as disclosure volume, while AI-powered phishing kits are bring sold SaaS-style to criminals with little technical skill, driving a measurable spike in BEC and ransomware activity.

Related:US Cyber Director Pick Signals Shift Toward Expertise

The two insurers also agree on where the real exposure lies: not necessarily with large enterprises, which have the resources to track threat intelligence and patch quickly, but with small and mid-sized businesses that often lack an in-house IT team altogether. That awareness gap, they argue, is where insurers now have to step in — not just to underwrite risk, but to actively help policyholders defend their organizations against it.

This transcript has been edited for clarity and length by Informa TechTarget's internal AI assistant. For the full experience, please listen to the podcast or watch the video.

Dark Reading's Becky Bracken: Hello, everyone, and welcome to Dark Reading Confidential, a podcast from the editors of Dark Reading, where we bring you real-world stories straight from the cyber trenches. My name is Becky Bracken. I'm an editor with Dark Reading, and I am thrilled today to do just that: deliver as promised, along with my colleague Rob Wright. Today we are going to be talking about all things cyber insurance and AI. In addition to my very smart colleague Rob Wright, who I mentioned, we are thrilled to welcome Lauren Winchester, who is vice president of cyber risk services at Travelers Insurance, and of course Daniel Woods, who is the head of emerging risk at Coalition. Welcome to both of you. Thank you.

Related:Behind the Alias: How One Researcher Unmasked a Decade of Ransomware Actors

DR's Becky Bracken: This conversation got started actually in one of our editorial meetings, and Rob had the smartest take, and then won the dubious distinction of getting to lead the conversation, which is always a problem around here when you have a smart idea. So, I am going to get out of the way and let Rob explain some of his very smart thinking around this topic, and get you guys in on it.

Dark Reading's Rob Wright: It wasn't a smart idea, but it was a question about what is happening with cyber insurance now that we have all this AI stuff going on. Thank you for joining me on this. We'll start with a basic question about where the cyber insurance industry is right now today in 2026, going into 2027. Just broad strokes. I know it's still somewhat of a new market, but where do you feel like it is in terms of overall health and growth? Daniel, why don't you kick us off?

Coalition's Daniel Woods: So I think the best data source on the state of the market comes from the National Association of Insurance Commissioners, who publish an annual report about the US market, and that shows this paradoxical situation where the number of policies being issued are growing, but the number or the amount of premium being collected is pretty much stable.

Related:In Security, Words Matter as Much as Tools — Maybe More

We also see claims are increasing; they rose 40% in 2024, 20% in 2025. So I think the cyber-insurance market is beginning to ask whether prices are sustainable, especially given some of the changes we'll discuss today related to AI.

DR's Rob Wright: And Lauren, what's your take on what the industry is today?

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: I think we're at a period of uncertainty right now, as most industries are. AI is likely to change a lot of the ways that we work, a lot of the ways that threat actors engage with companies and attack companies, and ultimately that uncertainty is something the industry is grappling with.

DR's Rob Wright: So I know I'm a bit of a pessimist among my Dark Reading colleagues. I try not to be pessimistic, but obviously AI has had a huge effect on the cybersecurity landscape this year. Ever since Project Glasswing, Mythos, and the tsunami of reported vulnerabilities, disclosed vulnerabilities, increases in zero-days. That's really put a lot of companies, a lot of vendors, and enterprises on the defensive. So, what effect is that having?

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: I think it's important not to conflate the surge in vulnerabilities being disclosed with a surge in attacks that drive cyber claims, at least not just yet. As many of your listeners will know, not all vulnerabilities are created equal, and so certainly there are thousands more vulnerabilities being disclosed in the months since Mythos preview came out, as compared to earlier in the year, and that's going to have an impact. And you have to look at the high-severity and the critical bugs within that group, and then you have to look at what's actually being exploited as well.

If we want to look at what's a good stand-in for exploitation, you can look at CISA's known exploited vulnerability list. There's a little lag there, but ultimately, if you look at the KEVs, you can see an increase that's happening, but it's not to the same order of magnitude as the overall CVE and vulnerability disclosure. But certainly, there is an increase. I think we just looked at this recently through Sept. 30, and CISA had added 246 KEVs in 2026, which exceeded the 245 added in total for 2025 when we looked at it. So naturally, we are on a trend to exceed last year.

We think maybe it'll be about a third more than last year, when we get to the end of the year, not a trend you want to see, but it's not in the same almost-exponential curve you're seeing with the total vulnerabilities. And I don't mean to be like everything's fine, right?

We also put out a report yesterday, and we look at ransomware leak site data as well as potential indicators of activity, and that's where ransomware threat actors are posting on their Dark Web leak sites who their victims are, and ultimately for Q3 2026, that activity was up. We had 111 distinct groups posting victims. We had a record number of 2,882 victims on leak sites, which is 26% up from the prior quarter and our highest quarter that we've ever seen. So certainly, the activity with the financially motivated threat actors is picking up, and we've also seen that in our business email compromises (BECs) as well, so it's painting a picture of more activity for sure. But just good to note that increased volumes don't necessarily mean increased tax immediately.

DR's Rob Wright: Gotcha. And I'll just say you are allowed to be positive on this podcast. I am going to actually implore you guys to give me some good news as we get deeper into this conversation because I always try to leave these discussions on a positive note, on a high note, rather than doom and gloom. So, we'll see if Daniel brings the doom and gloom now.

Coalition's Daniel Woods: So to bring a bit of gloom, I'd say the conversation also needs to look beyond just vulnerabilities and exploits. AI is a general-purpose technology, and it impacts not just the exploitation of vulnerabilities, but other mechanisms.

So Lauren mentioned an increase in business email compromise, which we've also seen, and we attribute this to in February of this year, a new AI phishing kit was released, EvilTokens, which was basically like a SaaS business model, and they weren't exploiting vulnerabilities. They were using AI to create convincing phishing lures, either by email or often replica websites. They would manipulate the user into letting the attacker in, even if MFA was enforced through either adversary in the middle and phishing, or device code phishing, and then the platform would get access to the mailbox, create mailbox rules, and start to pass through and identify sensitive data.

So this is a way that AI was used at multiple steps. It caused an increase in claims, but it's not actually related to the vulnerabilities that Lauren and you were just talking about. A perfect example. We also attribute that increase in BEC to those tools being made available to threat actors. They are truly too easy to use, unfortunately. EvilTokens was sold as a SaaS business model. So one sophisticated group of criminals built the technology, and they allowed tens, hundreds, thousands of criminals without those skills to use it. And a blockchain tracking company found that they earned $1.1 million in revenue from other criminals buying their services. So, I think this is what Coalition is really worried about when cybercriminals adopt AI into their business models.

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: We see that in the ransomware ecosystem, where there's ransomware-as-a-service (RaaS), and so part of what we're looking for is when will the tools become even further identified, and to what extent, who will be doing that? Will it be the ransomware developers? How will that actually get disseminated into the ecosystem? There's a little bit more separation between the original, the victims, and who actually ends up doing the attack at the end, because of the access broker economy and what have you. So, I agree with Daniel. You have to track how these different tools are getting used, and to what extent it has changed the ecosystem.

DR's Rob Wright: So I feel like we're trending in a direction that's less positive. That's OK. But going back to the vulnerabilities, do you feel like customers, like the enterprises out there that are buying insurance, are they aware of what's going on with the threat landscape? Do they know about the surge in reported vulnerabilities, and that the patching windows have decreased? That stuff is getting exploited a lot faster these days. That the risk is generally higher, and that there's a much bigger importance to promptly patching systems. Do they understand that, and do you see them acting on it?

Coalition's Daniel Woods: I would say there's a big range of customers. So, of course, at the high end, you have in the enterprise market customers with a team of threat intelligence specialists who don't really need an insurer to tell them about vulnerabilities being exploited. I think where you really see an awareness gap is small businesses. Many of them don't even have an IT team; they have a website some third-party developer built, and then you see something like the PHP vulnerability in that system, and that's where I think the onus is on the insurer to actively notify those customers that there is a new exposure for them. And that's where, in particular, what Coalition offers is support to actually help them patch those systems, because often for the very smallest customers, they don't have the capabilities to do it.

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: That's very well put. I think we're definitely doing our darndest to get the news out to our policyholders at Travelers because that is so important. We similarly have a book that runs the gamut from small businesses and mid-sized businesses, and then large enterprises, and the large enterprises are very aware. With middle market and small companies, I do think the work that we've done and other insurers have done to start that engagement before all of this began will hopefully pay off to some extent.

With our cyber-threat alerts, we're letting them know about any of these new emerging exploits, and then certainly in all of our monthly newsletters and quarterly webinars. This is all we can talk about, is how much faster companies need to be patching, and that their 30-day patch windows or SLAs with their vendors are not going to cut it in this new paradigm. They need to be looking to patch within three days, one day, even for certain types of exploits.

But it is interesting you mentioned PHP, not to have a side here, Daniel. But I find that one so fascinating, because the prevalence on the Web. A full 70% of websites could have PHP as a language within them. What do you do with that? You let everybody know about this vulnerability. You encourage everyone to patch, and I actually totally encourage all the press and the news around it because people were patching more quickly on this vulnerability. But turns out it's been pretty hard to exploit so far, right? And so that's a perfect situation for everyone. Something well-known, pervasive, get it fixed, and fortunately beat that exploitation.

DR's Rob Wright: In addition to shrinking patching windows, faster exploitation, and threat actors using AI to build more effective phishing and BEC attacks … what other risks or major threats do you see your clients facing today, as it pertains to AI?

Coalition's Daniel Woods: So, I would start with the rise of open source pen-testing frameworks like Artex. This was developed by a Chinese researcher, and it was actually later taken down because the researcher realized how much it was being abused. But what we've seen is this gives, very unsophisticated actors the ability to draw on more sophisticated pen testing tools that have many agents working in parallel to exploit publicly facing systems. What this pen-testing framework is able to do is test systems, test plug-ins, use a range of tools, and basically throw everything at the system and try every avenue, and the costs are so cheap.

For me, the really worrying thing here is often it's just people playing around. They don't even have the rationale on how to make money from this, and yet from a cyber-insurance perspective, you still have to respond to the claim if they steal data, which often happens. You still have to notify those customers. You'll face a lawsuit. So you have just chaos at the moment with these agentic hacking tools being used to just compromise so many different organizations.

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: And it's that experimentation that's happening right now. Once the financially motivated threat actors figure out how they want to harness that type of tool, that's when it becomes more concerning. I think right now we're in the early stages of it. That's translated into not seeing much examples of Artex used for ransomware yet, and they tend to be a little noisier right now, so to the point of the large enterprises who are really on top of things, they're going to identify that very quickly within their systems because it's so noisy.

But these tools are only going to get better, and it's something that looks for the existing vulnerabilities that are already out there, the existing exploits, and that's something that every corporation struggles with already. So again, back to the things we've been preaching for so long: needing to make sure you have good vulnerability management and patch management. That's how you combat these new tools. They're not doing net new ways to attack necessarily. They're using existing methods. They're just agentified and moving fast.

But the other risk, if we're talking about other risks from AI, I look at the inside threat of AI as well. here's a real gap between those that know they're adopting AI and those that have AI governance in place, and I think that's really huge. Whenever you have a technological step-change like AI is, adoption is going to get out ahead of governance, and that creates this risk. There's a lack of visibility for a lot of small businesses and middle-market businesses as to how employees are using these tools. There's a lack of accountability. There's a lack of governance. And we're not yet seeing claims and droves that result from this governance gap. But it's happening, and it's creating this risk, and so you can see how companies are going to start to have employees who put sensitive data into an unsanctioned AI tool.

We know that's happening, and that's going to create a potential data breach, depending on what type of information they're putting into the tool. So that's something that when we're engaging with policyholders right now, we're talking to them about vulnerability management, but we're turning around and trying to assess their actual governance risk as well.

DR's Rob Wright: Daniel, are you seeing similar trends in terms of just not necessarily shadow AI usage, but lack of AI governance?

Coalition's Daniel Woods: So, I for sure agree with Lauren. There's a bunch of organizations out there using unsanctioned tools. I think to Lauren's point, there's a bunch of data breaches happening that just will never be reported. They will never end up with a breach attorney who says your notification requirements are X, Y, and Z. I think at the moment, Coalition is more sanguine about that risk just because it's remaining in the shadows for now. I think we're really focused on the business email problem because that's causing the spiking claims at the moment, and also how organizations are going to deal with these open source pen-testing frameworks.

DR's Rob Wright: So a question about agentic AI. I've talked with some companies who are trying to find ways to use AI for good, trying to find vulnerabilities within their products, and develop patches in some cases. I spoke with Ivanti about this recently. Do you guys think that that's a realistic path? Have you, or is that a possibility? Like that we may be able to use some of this technology to remedy vulnerabilities, find vulnerabilities, and fix them before the attackers get them, or is that a long way off?

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: I think that's been happening. There are a lot of tech companies that are out ahead of the attackers because they've been running frontier models against their code bases, and then there are also companies that have been running against open source code, right? So there's a plethora of work happening right now to shore up code to ship new patches.

I think one of the problems, though, is for the companies that don't have massive teams who can work on establishing patches. You're told about a new vulnerability. How do you turn around and action that and ship a patch that doesn't have more bugs, right? And so I think that's the spiral we're in right now. There's a bit of a bottleneck, and then companies who are consumers of those tech products then have to do the patches in a lot of cases if it's not a simple cloud update. So, I see this bottleneck happening right now, but it is certainly out ahead of attackers.

That said, some of the models that are out there and in use by attackers now are more sophisticated. So we're at that crux point now, where there are models out there that threat actors can use to identify vulnerabilities and create exploits. And the clock's been ticking, and we're at that intersection.

Coalition's Daniel Woods: Exactly to Lauren's point, there will always be another model that can find new vulnerabilities. Something we're concerned by is, there's been a recent spate of outages. Of course, everyone heard about Microsoft last week. The FAA had an outage, and there's noise that potentially the speed at which organizations are now having to develop and deploy patches is leading to some of these outages. Just because you have to test all of them, and eventually something goes wrong. You test one configuration, then it's pushed to a different server, and that's what causes the outage.

So, although potentially defenders will be able to stay ahead in terms of patching the vulnerabilities, the big concern for cyber insurance is that leads to reliability issues that cause systems failure and business interruptions, which we also cover. So you might solve the security risk, and increase the outage risk.

DR's Rob Wright: I guess the thing I worry about again, and I am a pessimist, so take this with a grain of salt, but is the number of organizations out there that generate the code with AI tools, and push it live without not a lot of human review or really any human review.

And I worry about a future where we're going to have Patch Tuesdays with not just 900 or 1,000 vulnerabilities, but 2,000 or more, and we're just going to be running the code through these AI tools and pushing them out because the sheer number is just too big, and Daniel to your point, not having the quality in place to effectively fix the vulnerability, and also stuff that might screw things up, and you might have outages. That's the panic mode in my brain. Is that something that you guys are worried about, or do you think that we might have the human capability, the human workforce to actually review these patches and the code, and get them in a place that they should be, rather than just pushing them out willy-nilly?

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: I think it's important to not talk about it too broadly, right? Tech companies creating tech have an obligation, a responsibility to have software development lifecycles, so it's one thing to be running AI against the code to test it for vulnerabilities and identify them. You need to make sure you have the proper harnesses in place and the proper engineers and humans overseeing that process, and then you want to run it still before it ships to look for vulnerabilities. You need that life cycle. If they're not doing that, that's creating a real problem. But responsible companies who are creating tech responsibly still have their rigorous software development life cycles in place.

Coalition's Daniel Woods: And I think where I would take that is a similar direction. The cyber insurance industry has seen a huge increase in claims related to SSL VPNs provided by a handful of providers who built them in legacy, proprietary operating systems, they never rewrote those that codebase with modern secure software engineering models. And honestly, I think that business model will always be stuck in this reactive cycle. I think what the insurance industry needs to move toward, and it's something Coalition is doing, is shifting organizations toward a more modern approach to developing software, where you build security in early in the process.

So, for instance, a huge amount of vulnerabilities are related to how memory is managed. There are by now completely memory-safe programming languages that vendors can use to build their systems. So, I think the insurance industry needs to move toward rewarding policyholders who are using more modern technology sets. So, we escape the patch cycle because it's just not sustainable to be telling people at 5 p.m. on a Friday you need to patch your systems. Our staff get exhausted from this because they stay up all night notifying people, and then also the people receiving those emails are just as exhausted.

DR's Rob Wright: You mentioned VPNs. I wanted to ask you guys: so obviously, edge devices have been under attack now for a long time, and there have been specific companies and products that have been favorite targets of threat actors. Lots of vulnerabilities, zero-days, etc. Are carriers or the insurance companies approaching a point where you guys might say to a customer, you know what? If you have this in your environment, the risk is too high for us, and just walk away or say that it's a no-go because of certain edge devices or edge products in that environment? Or are we not there yet?

Coalition's Daniel Woods: So I would say, for the vast majority, you can build compensating controls in. So we know SSL VPNs are not great. What we would do is work with the customer to update the firmware to the latest version to enforce multifactor authentication or MFA, and then what we would also ask is whether they have running behind that, a modern managed detection and response solution. So if something gets through, you at least detect it and contain it quickly. It's quite rare that we say there's no way we can insure you. It's more about working with the customer, so they're secure enough to offer terms to.

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: We take a similar approach as well. We're certainly identifying at the point of underwriting some of the more risky technologies, so that those conversations can be had, those questions can be asked. I'm reaching out to help them understand what a proper patching cadence is going to look like with that edge device that's constantly under attack. What are their plans to move toward something that, in the future, will be more secure? It's not always realistic for a company to move off their VPN quickly, and we recognize that. So it's for those that lean in and say how can I do this securely, and then how can I roadmap to get to where we need to be in the future.

DR's Rob Wright: Last question on AI for you guys. So, we've covered threat actors using AI tools. We've covered the pen testing tools that are available, frameworks, and what it has done to the influx of vulnerabilities. But what about rogue models, agents escaping containment, agents getting into other networks; is that something that you guys have gamed out plans for? Like, if a client is breached by way of an escaped agent, and there's damage or there's data exposed, does it matter who's behind a breach for your customers, or does it become a different calculus when AI is involved?

Coalition's Daniel Woods: So, from a coverage perspective, the way cyber insurance policies are worded, the coverage trigger is a security failure, and it's about the victim organization and some unauthorized action being taken on their network.

We never say, we'll cover these kinds of attacks, but not these kinds of attackers. It's always just been about the victim, what did the security incident look like? I don't think this is a coverage conversation.

I think historically the media, and I know we're on a podcast at the moment, has focused on certain kinds of attacks that make great stories, and I think this message that OpenAI was developing a model and it escaped: it makes a great story, but the reality is, for the vast majority of businesses, they're not going to face that kind of threat. I think it's incumbent on cyber insurance to use our data to bring a more sober conversation and focus on how they will actually be compromised: business email compromise, funds transfer fraud, ransomware, and I think that's what we should be helping businesses prevent.

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: I agree. I think when we talk about the motivations of a rogue agent, it's different than our financially motivated threat actors potentially who are looking to capitalize on that attack. So what that agent does within the environment dictates the response. So, like Daniel said, it doesn't matter if it's a human or an agent. What happened within the environment? What access was had? What data was accessed or exfiltrated or locked up?

I do think, though, there are concerns about agentic use within an organization and when there's agentic drift as well, and that's certainly a known risk, especially among companies that are developing tech. And there are good solutions starting to be built and put into market to help with that monitoring of agents.

But there again, right now in this moment, a lot of companies are lacking that insight and that visibility and that monitoring of agentic behavior, so it's a risk but not something that we're necessarily seeing pronounced in claims yet.

DR's Rob Wright: OK, so let's take a beat and look at 2027. Let's close it out with a look down the road. What do you guys see happening in the next year for the cybersecurity industry, just broadly, Daniel?

Coalition's Daniel Woods: I would expect the increase in frequency of the attacks we've spoken about: business email compromise, phone transfer fraud. The latest evaluations of the very frontier models are finding they can't consistently go from the perimeter of a network to full domain compromise. So I don't think we're going to see automated end-to-end ransomware in 2027. That said, I think there's going to be a lot of chaos, a lot of Web-facing systems breached. They often contain credit-card data, email addresses. So I think it's going to look a lot like the 2010s, more like data breaches, business email compromise.

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: If we think about the types of claims that can really drive costs, to what extent are those attacks becoming more and more identified as agentic? Because that necessarily allows threat actors who are financially motivated to have more efficiency and to go after more companies potentially.

Unfortunately, the ransomware threat actors are very successful right now with what they have. And if it ain't broke, don't fix it. So we fully anticipate to see more agentification of different aspects of the attack chain, but that full chaining would be a significant change in this landscape.

DR's Rob Wright: Right. I feel a little bit better about where we are based on what you guys are telling me. Sobering news about how this is going to look more like the 2010s, and we're not going to be having Skynet attacks of rogue agents and models running amok, Daniel. I think you're probably right about that. It's reassuring to hear it. But thank you guys for weighing in on this and giving us a look at what's happening in your industry. Appreciate it.

DR's Becky Bracken: I was going to say, the 2010s don't sound really bad at this point, frankly.

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: We had some pretty massive data breaches back then, so I wouldn't seem too eager to go back to that pattern.

DR's Becky Bracken: Oh, something about a BlackBerry though just brought me a sense of comfort. I wanted just to ask, because I am infinitely smarter after this conversation than I was on this topic. So thank you all so much, Rob, for your smart questions and Daniel and Lauren. Your wealth of expertise is invaluable. But I want to ask the question that we started off with, which is: are premiums sustainable? Is the cost of cybersecurity insurance in its current stratosphere, is it sustainable? And what is going to happen with costs in your estimation?

Coalition's Daniel Woods: I don't think the current trend is sustainable, and I think premiums will have to go up. If there are more claims, there are more costs. Insurers need to collect the premiums to cover it.

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: And I think that statement was true prior to the frontier AI models. So a lot of insurers within the industry are definitely feeling that that struggle with the current levels of premiums and the current view of risk.

DR's Rob Wright: Although I gotta say, you would think they'd be much higher than they are right now. So I guess that's good. I can't believe I'm being an optimist right now.

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: There are other market conditions at play. There are a lot of markets that are selling cyber, and there are new entrants, and that has an impact.

DR's Becky Bracken: Again, you guys have left us all with a lot to think about, from the governance gap to the speed up of patching introducing configuration problems. I think that we're going to be thinking a lot about all of this in the future.

Daniel Woods, head of emerging risk at Coalition; Lauren Winchester, head of cyber risk services at Travelers Insurance; and Rob Wright, senior news director at Dark Reading: thank you all so much for your time and your expertise today, we appreciate it. This has been Dark Reading Confidential, a podcast from the editors of Dark Reading, bringing you real-world stories straight from the cyber trenches. I'm Becky Bracken, on behalf of all of us at Dark Reading. Thank you so much for tuning in, and we will see you or hear you next time. Bye.

The Dark Reading Editorial Team consists of Kelly Jackson Higgins, Fahmida Y Rashid, Tara Seals, Rob Wright, Becky Bracken, Alex Culafi, Arielle Waldman, and Kristina Beek. Among us, we have over 99 years of experience covering cybersecurity. That's pretty striking considering the industry hasn't even been around that long.

Mourinho, punto e basta. Così Jude Bellingham ha risposto ai giornalisti che gli hanno chiesto dei suoi miglioramenti. Il giocatore inglese del Real Madrid dà quindi tanto merito all'ex allenatore dell'Inter. Alla fine della partita di Champions contro il PSV, il calciatore in zona mista ha parlato della vittoria, del suo gol e dei suoi 45 minuti più incisivi nell'esperienza al Real, come dicono in Spagna.

Scegli Fcinter1908.it come tuo sito preferito su Google: clicca qui

Ha pure parlato del suo rinnovo 'imminente' secondo alcune fonti con il club spagnolo: «Non ne ho parlato con la società. Non ne ho parlato con la società. Ho visto la notizia sui sui social e ho chiesto ai miei genitori... 'È vero o no? Comunque sono felice qui. Certo, certo. Sto giocando bene, mi diverto a giocare a calcio. Adoro vivere qui in Spagna. Per me, in questo momento, in questo club è tutto perfetto. Il mio contratto, il rinnovo... è ancora lontano».

Il Real Madrid perdeva alla fine del primo tempo, uno a zero, aveva segnato l'ex Inter Ivan Perisic su rigore, poi è uscita dagli spogliatoi un'altra squadra: «Se sono all'apice della mia carriera? Potrebbe essere. Mi diverto molto a giocare a calcio. Adoro Mourinho, il suo staff ... i miei compagni di squadra mi aiutano molto. Ed è per questo che sto giocando bene, perché ricevo tanto supporto da tutti. Non so se per come gioco. Perché mi muovo molto per arretrare e aiutare a costruire le azioni offensive, così come per spingermi in avanti e finalizzare. Mourinho mi dà molta fiducia, ed è per questo che sto giocando bene, perché sono molto contento», ha spiegato ai giornalisti.

−Qual è stato il segreto del tuo miglioramento?

−Cosa ha detto Mourinho nell'intervallo? Era molto arrabbiato?

Sì, un po' arrabbiato. Ma è normale. È un allenatore di altissimo livello che si aspetta molto di più da noi. E penso che siamo migliorati molto nel secondo tempo. Credo che dobbiamo essere più concentrati dall'inizio della partita. A volte non basta giocare bene nel secondo tempo; è importante anche iniziare le partite in modo rapido, deciso e concentrato. Altrimenti, possiamo faticare nelle partite importanti.

Molte cose. L'atmosfera... è un allenatore che non pensa solo al calcio, ma per lui è importante come si sentono i giocatori. E questo per noi significa molto. Sentiamo di avere qualcuno che vuole sempre lottare per noi. Ed è per questo che lottiamo per lui e per il club. Perché siamo molto contenti.

Non è pressione, no. È un onore. La pressione sta nel vincere le partite. Faccio cose che altri pensano siano simili a quelle di Zidane, ma lui era molto più bravo di me.

Romano: "Varela via già a gennaio? Inter, ecco perché non ha ancora affondato. Non si esclude..."

Fabrizio Romano ha ulteriormente approfondito una situazione di mercato seguita dall'Inter. Il giornalista esperto di calciomercato ha approfondito lo scenario relativo a Varela, attaccante del Monza che piace all'Inter ma non solo. A Scommesse.io Romano ha ricordato: "Il calciomercato di gennaio è sempre più vicino e c’è già chi si muove, non solo ai piani più alti della classifica. C’è chi lotta per non retrocedere, un mercato di idee che spesso va anticipato pur lasciando spazio alle occasioni last minute di una finestra veloce come quella invernale. In prima fila c’è il futuro di un uomo solo al comando: Gustavo Varela è la stella più inaspettata di questo inizio di stagione in Serie A, una perla scovata dal Monza per poco più di 2 milioni di euro e oggi nella lista dei top club dopo i suoi gol no-stop in questi primi tre mesi di stagione. "Il Napoli ha già incontrato i suoi agenti, i rapporti sono eccellenti col direttore Manna dopo le operazioni Rafa Marin e Alisson Santos con la stessa agenzia. La Juve si è informata sul prezzo: il Monza per una cifra intorno ai 30/35 milioni di euro può davvero vendere Varela già a gennaio, attenzione perché il prezzo è fissato e i club stranieri iniziano a fiutare l'idea e scrutare i progressi di Gustavo, non possiamo escludere blitz e colpi di scena. Dovesse arrivare la cessione, si tratterebbe di una grave perdita che potrebbe intaccare le quote salvezza del Monza", ha concluso il giornalista.

From Files to Cited Answers in One API Call: Managed Ingestion and SearchDocuments in Agent Retrieval

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A guide to the new managed ingestion and SearchDocuments features in Agent Retrieval, for searching PDFs, Office files, and videos without writing an ingestion pipeline.

Before an agent can answer a question about your PDFs, someone has to build the pipeline: parse every file, split it into chunks, embed each chunk, write the chunks to a vector database, and then learn that database's schema to query it. For video it gets harder: transcode the files, cut them into clips, embed the frames, and keep track of which second of which video each clip came from.

Most of that code has nothing to do with your application. Two features recently added to Agent Retrieval (formerly Vector Search 2.0), now in Public Preview, take that work off your hands. Managed ingestion builds the pipeline for you: point a data store at files in Cloud Storage, and the service parses, segments, and embeds them with Gemini Embedding 2. SearchDocuments, the second new feature, then searches the data store with one API call and returns the matching passages with their page numbers or timestamps. In our tests, 18 Alphabet investor PDFs went from a public Cloud Storage bucket to searchable, page cited results in about 9 minutes, and 19 minutes of video became searchable by both visuals and spoken audio in 0.3 to 0.5 seconds per query, with no parsing, chunking, transcription, or embedding code.

This guide covers how to create a data store, import documents and videos, and search them with filters, reranking, and audio-aware video search.

Pairing managed ingestion with SearchDocuments gives you several immediate benefits:

Agent Retrieval Collections already give you semantic, text, and hybrid search over your own data objects. But you have to produce those data objects yourself, and to query them you need to know how they are stored. Here is what changes when you let a data store do the work:

The backing Collection is still a regular Agent Retrieval Collection, so you can query it directly whenever you need full control.

A data store holds your files and the configuration that controls how they are parsed and segmented. The data store and SearchDocuments APIs are currently served over REST at v1beta, so the examples use an authorized session from google-auth:

Two segmentation modes are available. Chunk segmentation (the default) splits text into chunks of 100 to 500 tokens and works with layout parsing, which also detects tables and headings. Page segmentation produces one segment per page. For paginated documents such as PDFs, both modes record page numbers (startPage and endPage) so you can cite pages or filter by page range: page segmentation returns a single page per segment, while chunk segmentation returns the page range each chunk spans.

Point an import at your files with URI patterns such as gs://my-bucket/reports/*.pdf, or with a JSONL manifest when you want to set your own document IDs and attach metadata:

The import runs as a long-running operation. Re-importing the same files updates existing documents instead of duplicating them, and files whose content didn't change are skipped.

SearchDocuments needs only the data store and a text query:

Each result is a segment of a document, with the text, the page, and the source file:

Pass filter to search only the segments that match, using the same paths you read back in results:

You can also pass document_ids to search only inside specific files, and output_fields.metadata to return each document's metadata with its results. In our test, searching "What did the CEO say about AI?" with the page-1 filter returned the CEO quotes from the headline page of five different earnings releases.

For "How many employees does Alphabet have?", plain hybrid search returned two balance sheet pages among the top five, which don't mention employees at all. With reranking on, every top result reported the headcount: the annual report's "Alphabet had 190,234 employees" and the "Number of employees" row of the quarterly supplemental tables.

Video works the same way: create a data store with document_type: VIDEO, import the files, and call SearchDocuments. The service transcodes each video, splits it into fixed windows, and embeds each window:

Each result carries the moment it matched:

We imported five Google sample videos (about 19 minutes in total) and searched them with scene descriptions. "Cucumbers being sorted by a machine" returned 03:20-03:40 of the machine learning video, where a farm's sorting machine grades cucumbers, without any tags or captions. Searches returned in 0.3 to 0.5 seconds.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

By default (audio_mode: DISABLED), only the frames are embedded, so a query matches what is shown. With ENABLED, the first audio track is embedded together with the frames. We imported the same videos into two data stores and asked both about statements from a keynote, spoken while the picture shows the speaker:

Only the audio-enabled data store found the right moments.

The rule of thumb: If your data is files, let a data store ingest them and search with SearchDocuments. Drop down to the Collection only when you need control the data store doesn't give you.

Managed ingestion doesn't change what good retrieval needs: well-segmented content, good embeddings, and the right filters. What changes is who builds it. Instead of maintaining parsers, chunkers, embedding jobs, and a schema, you point a data store at a bucket and get back segments that are ready to cite.

For agents, that means a retrieval tool that returns where each answer came from, a page in a PDF or a moment in a video, with one API call.

Try it yourself: the document notebook ingests 18 Alphabet investor PDFs and walks through filters and reranking, and the video notebook searches five sample videos with an inline player and compares audio modes.

CD Projekt Red signs new marketing partnership deal with Sony for The Witcher 4, and to increase "the presence" of its games on PlayStation

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

As part of The Witcher and Cyberpunk studio CD Projekt Red's recent Q3 earnings report, the developer offered some updates on upcoming game The Witcher 4.

During an earnings call yesterday, the studio revealed it had signed a new partnership deal with Sony regarding marketing rights to The Witcher 4, as well as to extend "the presence" of CD Projekt Red's other games across the PlayStation Store and PlayStation Plus.

"Just today, we signed new partnership agreements with Sony Interactive Entertainment covering the presence of our games on PlayStation and in PlayStation Plus subscriptions, as well as our collaboration during the upcoming Witcher 4 marketing campaign," said studio CFO Piotr Nielubowicz. "This cooperation will support our business and financial outlook in this and future years."

Nielubowicz didn't elaborate on what exactly that means for The Witcher 4's marketing, but perhaps it could enable Sony to advertise the action-RPG as a PlayStation game, similar to the Grand Theft Auto 6 'plays best on PlayStation' campaign. But seeing as The Witcher 4 is currently targeting a 2028 release, we may not find out what form that agreement takes for some time.

That said, looking at similar marketing agreements from Sony's recent history, we can probably expect The Witcher 4 to show up during Sony State of Play presentations or even have dedicated PlayStation-focused showcases. The Witcher 4 footage may also be specified as being captured on PlayStation hardware - something we already saw with The Witcher 4 tech demo last year. Perhaps we'll see Witcher-themed accessories and PlayStation hardware, too.

This is a notable shift for CDPR, which has historically had close ties to Xbox. Cyberpunk 2077's big Keanu Reeves reveal (as Johnny Silverhand) took place on Xbox's E3 2019 stage, and The Witcher 3's reveal happened on Microsoft's E3 2013 stage. Going further back, Xbox 360 was the only console The Witcher 2 was released on.

During the same earnings call, CDPR joint-CEO Michał Nowakowski said that we should expect the leap between The Witcher 3 and The Witcher 4 to be similar to that between GTA 5 and GTA 6, while also praising Rockstar's approach to marketing its record-breaking new game.

An annoying ‘mistake’ in macOS was actually prep for the latest MacBook Pro

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

You may remember that Apple made a seeming mistake in the user interface for macOS 26, meaning that attempts to resize windows would often fail. The reason for this has now become clear, and Apple has done something both fun and clever in macOS 27 to resolve it …

I was one of a number of people to note that trying to grab the corner of a window in macOS Tahoe in order to resize it would often fail. Developer Norbert Heger pointed out this is because Apple changed the radius of the corners without changing the grabbable area.

It turns out that my initial click in the window corner instinctively happens in an area where the window doesn’t respond to it. The window expects this click to happen in an area of 19 × 19 pixels, located near the window corner. If the window had no rounded corners at all, 62% of that area would lie inside the window. But due to the huge corner radius in Tahoe, most of it – about 75% – now lies outside the window.

At the time, the radius change itself looked like an inexplicable mistake on Apple’s part, and the company did revert it. However, Macworld’s Michael Simon appears to have discovered the reason for the original change.

The new rounder corners on the MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR are basically a perfect match for the macOS 26 radii.

So if you move a window to the corner of the new machine, it would fit perfectly. However, since Apple reverted the corner radius, it would no longer do so. The company solved this by a clever UI transition.

What’s happening there is Golden Gate’s 20-point corner radius is subtly and gradually expanding when It approaches the bottom corner of the screen to match the exact corner of the display, which is much larger. I can’t be certain it’s exactly 26-point, but it sure looks like it to me. The effect is incredible. I’ve watched it like 20 times and I can barely see it changing, yet when it reaches the corner it’s perfectly expanded to match.

You can see this illustrated by a GIF in the Macworld piece. While the original change annoyed me, Apple’s solution is the perfect example of the company sweating the details.

VanEck is dropping Gemini from nearly $1 billion in Bitcoin, Ethereum and Solana ETFs

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Coinbase will remain the funds' custodian as VanEck ends its custody, clearing and staking arrangements with Gemini.

VanEck is ending its relationship with Gemini across three spot crypto ETFs, leaving Coinbase Custody as its remaining named custodian.

The asset manager disclosed in Nov. 19 regulatory filings that it had notified Gemini of plans to terminate its custody and clearing agreements for its Bitcoin ETF (HODL), Ethereum ETF (ETHV) and Solana ETF (VSOL).

The agreements are scheduled to expire in February 2027, and Gemini will continue to provide services until the termination takes effect. The decision also ends a separate arrangement under which Gemini facilitates Solana staking for VSOL.

The filings did not disclose the reasons for the termination or how much cryptocurrency VanEck currently holds with Gemini.

The three funds have attracted approximately $1.15 billion in cumulative net inflows since their respective launches and collectively managed $934.2 million in net assets as of Nov. 24, according to SoSoValue data.

VanEck's Bitcoin ETF accounted for the bulk of that exposure, with $977.1 million in cumulative net inflows and $848.2 million in net assets. Its Ethereum and Solana funds recorded cumulative net inflows of $151.6 million and $19.2 million, respectively.

VanEck's decision extends beyond asset safekeeping. Gemini also provides clearing infrastructure that helps the funds settle cryptocurrency transactions associated with ETF share creations and redemptions.

Under the existing arrangements, VanEck's funds can use Gemini Clearing to complete trades negotiated with liquidity providers that are also Gemini customers.

The service has supported cash-based orders, which require the funds to purchase or sell cryptocurrencies, and in-kind transactions involving the direct transfer of digital assets.

The scheduled February 2027 exit affects two services across VanEck’s HODL, ETHV and VSOL.

Coinbase Custody stays with all three funds. The filings name no successor to Gemini’s trade clearing service.

The lines track service appointments. The filings give no amount or timing for any transfers between custodians.

The connections trace each service described in the three November 19 termination filings across its scheduled effective date.

The lines track service appointments. The filings give no amount or timing for any transfers between custodians.

Coinbase will continue serving the Bitcoin and Ethereum funds under their existing prime broker agreements, while the Solana fund also maintains a separate custody agreement with the company.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

VanEck did not identify a successor to Gemini's clearing services or provide a timetable for any cryptocurrency transfers between the custodians.

VanEck's decision contrasts with arrangements at other major US crypto ETF issuers that have established relationships with multiple digital asset custodians.

BlackRock's iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF (IBIT) uses Coinbase Custody as its custodian and has appointed Anchorage Digital Bank as an additional provider. Its latest quarterly report said the fund had no immediate plans to transfer Bitcoin to Anchorage.

BlackRock also maintains flexibility in its trading arrangements. The fund can execute Bitcoin transactions directly with third-party counterparties or through Coinbase Prime when processing cash-based creation and redemption orders.

Meanwhile, the Fidelity Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund maintains its primary custody and trade execution relationship with Fidelity Digital Assets, an affiliate of its sponsor. Fidelity has also appointed BitGo Bank & Trust as an additional custodian, although it reported no immediate plans to transfer Bitcoin to the company.

These arrangements allow BlackRock and Fidelity to retain additional custody providers without necessarily distributing their existing Bitcoin holdings across them.

VanEck's termination of Gemini's agreements moves in the opposite direction across three separate crypto investment products, increasing the importance of its remaining relationship with Coinbase.

Its September quarterly reports describe transactions conducted directly with liquidity providers, while earlier prospectuses also allowed alternative execution arrangements for certain trades if settlement failed.

Those existing arrangements could accommodate transactions previously handled through Gemini Clearing without VanEck appointing another dedicated clearing provider.

With the termination scheduled for February, VanEck's subsequent regulatory disclosures could reveal whether it intends to consolidate more of its trading infrastructure with Coinbase or establish new arrangements with other institutional service providers.

In The Air & On The Ground: How Premium Demand Has Rewritten Airline Operations

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Surging demand for premium airfare has forced global airlines to rewrite how they operate both inside the cabin and at the airport terminal. Customer demand passed a decisive juncture when Delta Air Lines reported that it made more 2025 Q4 revenue from premium-cabin sales than from main cabin fares for the first time. The landmark shift came as North America and Asia were already tearing out their economy rows while pouring billions into new business suites, satellite WiFi, exclusive lounges, and more perks.

That universal push to capture big spenders collided directly with global supply chain bottlenecks, rising oil costs, and certification delays. United Airlines launched its Elevated Boeing 787-9 with Polaris suite doors locked open pending federal sign-off, while Delta installed 44 domestic first class recliners aboard new Airbus A321neos after planned lie-flat pods missed testing. Overseas, Boeing's delayed 777-9 forced Emirates, Singapore Airlines, and Air Canada to spend billions rebuilding older widebodies to keep premium capacity expanding.

Every carrier has chosen a unique strategy to balance luxury inflight with terminal perks as passenger spending shifts. In another landmark change, United and American Airlines retired international first class while rolling out oversized business cabins and co-branded credit card clubs, while Emirates and Singapore kept enclosed first class suites alive alongside expanded hub lounges. Even US low-cost carriers scheduled domestic first class seats and airport clubs for 2027, leaving basic economy fliers with tighter legroom and fewer perks.

Delta Chief Executive Officer Ed Bastian explained that the second-quarter milestone by confirming nearly all new seat growth now targets business class and premium economy fliers. American CEO Robert Isom echoed that shift on September 16, admitting his carrier sold too much Basic Economy when just 30% of seats generate half its revenue. Isom told View From The Wing: "Those 30% of seats, they're only going to grow in our fleet. That's demand that's more likely to hold up through economic uncertainty."

Delta saw immediate proof of that appetite aboard its accidental 44-seat first class A321neo subfleet flying between Hartsfield-Jackson Atlanta, Los Angeles, and Seattle. Even with undersized galleys and a single forward lavatory, paying passengers filled 80% of those recliners by August. As Delta Chief Commercial Officer Joe Esposito put it to From The Tray Table: "It's a really nice surprise that we've got such high demand for our premium products. We're experimenting with how big a domestic premium cabin can be."

American backed Isom's words by removing elite upgrades and frequent-flyer miles from Basic Economy tickets purchased after mid-May, and by using its cheapest fare to direct passengers into more expensive rows. Executives also announced Flagship First's retirement on March 28, 2027, bringing narrowbody premium seating to 40%. Air Canada targeted those same corporate travelers by relocating Boeing 737 MAX 8 jets to Rouge's leisure arm, while reinstating Airbus A320-200 and A321-200 aircraft to regular service.

Air Canada introduced lie-flat beds to single-aisle transatlantic flights in June, when it launched its first Airbus A321XLR between Montreal and Toronto before deploying the jet to Toulouse. Alaska and Hawaiian Airlines joined the narrowbody luxury drive later in the year, beginning in 2028 with lie-flat suites on upcoming Boeing 737 MAX 10 aircraft and upgraded Airbus A330-200s. Putting long-haul comfort on smaller jets allows carriers to serve narrower foreign markets profitably while avoiding widebody fuel use.

Airlines used different strategies to rearrange their cabins to accommodate large spenders on widebody fleets. In September, American launched its 'Project Olympus' Boeing 777-300ER between New York-JFK and Buenos Aires, eliminating international first class in favor of crammed Flagship Suites. In a similar vein, United expanded Polaris business class and Premium Plus capacity by eliminating dozens of regular economy seats on its Elevated Boeing 787-9.

Even as they rebuilt their fleets, foreign competitors refused to give up on the very front of the aircraft. In July, Emirates launched the 100th aircraft from its $5 billion interior makeover in Dubai. It preserved the enclosed first class suites on all of its Airbus A380-800 and 777-300ER planes while making room for premium economy. With the addition of private first class suites to its ultra-long-range Airbus A350-900ULR aircraft operating marathon runs to New York-JFK, Singapore pushed its own widebody rebuild budget to more than $1.56 billion.

Every square inch added to the front of these widebodies forced uncomfortable compromises behind the curtain. Singapore ran into sharp flier pushback after revealing that its retrofitted A350-900s keep older 2017 economy seats without 4K screens or Bluetooth audio. On incoming Boeing 777-9 deliveries, Singapore is also tightening standard economy from nine to 10 abreast in the upcoming 777X, trading away long-praised coach comfort to fund upfront luxury.

Delta Air Lines and United Airlines are producing record results on the back of a surge in premium travel.

As jetliners evolve, airport clubs are becoming increasingly congested as US airlines aggressively entice frequent travelers with co-branded credit cards. In September, Southwest Airlines and Chase announced that their first-ever Icon Lounges would debut in Austin, Baltimore, Honolulu, and Nashville in late 2027. The newly merged Alaska and Hawaiian also announced plans to open new flagship clubs in Honolulu and Seattle in 2028, directly challenging bank-run establishments such as the Chase Sapphire Lounge at Phoenix Sky Harbor.

Alaska Chief Executive Officer Ben Minicucci pitched those ground investments as a core part of the travel experience, saying: "We believe premium is more than a seat. It's how guests feel from the moment they book their trip to the moment they arrive at the airport to when they're in the air with us." On the same thread, to keep top international ticket holders out of packed credit card queues, Air Canada opened a seventh grab-and-go café in Québec City in June while offering apron Porsche transfers from its Signature Suites.

Singapore took a similar approach at its home hub, spending $34.1 million to expand its Changi Terminal 2 SilverKris lounges by 50% and give paid premium fliers dedicated space away from partner cardholders. Singapore Chief Executive Officer Goh Choon Phong said: "Our customers experience Singapore as one seamless journey, and this significant investment is designed to deliver greater comfort, choice, and convenience at every stage."

As construction continues, high-speed satellite internet has become just as essential to winning premium bookings as private terminal clubs. United raced ahead fleet-wide, passing 640 SpaceX Starlink-equipped aircraft in October to lead US carriers in total connected jets. Emirates prioritized raw long-haul bandwidth instead, mounting twin fuselage antennas on every Boeing 777-300ER and three atop each Airbus A380-800, so hundreds of passengers can stream simultaneously above 2 gigabits per second.

Premium cabin demand has only continued to grow.

In February, Capital One stopped offering free guest and authorized user access on its Venture X card. Instead, it now charges $45 per adult guest unless the member spends $75,000 annually. Even though over 70 new lounges started construction this year, banks and airlines tightened admission requirements due to persistent overcrowding. A few days after restricting access, Capital A's 12,500-square-foot (1,161 sqm) facility was opened, arriving at LaGuardia Airport with faster table turnover and custom-made tapas.

Alaska and Hawaiian separated their terminal spaces between domestic crowds and long-haul flyers. Hawaiian broke ground in October on a 13,000-square-foot (1,208 sq m) Honolulu lounge for 200 guests as part of a $600 million overhaul. Alaska simultaneously advanced a 41,000-square-foot (3,809 sq m) Seattle complex for late 2027, splitting a 500-seat standard club from a 200-seat Aurora Lounge for international business-class fliers.

United and American answered bank-run clubs with giant hub expansions and quick bypass lanes. United pushed ahead on the 40,000-square-foot (3,716 sq m), 650-seat United Club in Washington Dulles Concourse E after finishing near the bottom of the recent J.D. Power satisfaction rankings. American countered at New York's JFK Terminal 8 with a quick-service coffee and snack counter while reopening its Los Angeles Flagship First Dining room.

Found an error? Send it info@simpleflying.com so it can be corrected.

The next major test for airline ground operations arrives between late 2027 and 2028, when Southwest's Icon Lounges, Alaska's two-tier Seattle complex, and Hawaiian's Honolulu flagship open their doors. Adding thousands of square feet of terminal space will only ease congestion if airlines and credit card issuers keep tightening guest rules. Tracking those openings will grant a glimpse into the next stage of competition in the terminal.

What is MailBaby? Outbound SMTP relay pricing, limits and how it works

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 25 • Originally published at srvscripts.com

Running outbound mail from a shared hosting IP is a reputation game that most providers lose slowly. One compromised contact form can undo months of clean sending, and getting an IP off a major blocklist costs engineering hours every time. MailBaby is InterServer’s answer to that problem: an outbound-only SMTP smarthost that takes every message your server sends, scores it, and delivers it from IP pools whose reputation InterServer manages. This guide explains what the service does, what it costs, and the constraints you need to design around before pointing a production server at it.

Short answer: MailBaby is InterServer’s outbound-only SMTP smarthost: a hosting server authenticates to relay.mailbaby.net on port 25 with STARTTLS, every message is spam-scored, and clean mail is delivered from reputation-managed IP pools while bad mail is bounced back. It costs $1 per month for about 5,000 messages plus $0.20 per 1,000 after that, caps sending at 6,000 messages per hour per address, and leaves inbound mail, MX records and Dovecot untouched. (Reviewed October 2026.)

In short: MailBaby is InterServer’s outbound-only SMTP smarthost: a hosting server authenticates to relay.mailbaby.net on port 25 with STARTTLS, every message is spam-scored, and clean mail is delivered from reputation-managed IP pools while bad mail is bounced back.

MailBaby sits between your mail server and the internet. Exim, Postfix or Plesk authenticates to relay.mailbaby.net on port 25 with STARTTLS, hands over the message, and MailBaby takes responsibility for delivery. Every message passes through a content filter that assigns a spam score. Clean mail is routed out through a sending pool matched to the reputation of the sending account; borderline mail goes through a lower-reputation pool; mail that scores badly is bounced back to your server with a rejection such as “classified as rSPAM” so the sender knows it was not delivered.

Because InterServer has run the platform on its own infrastructure since May 2024, the scoring model, the IP pools and the feedback-loop handling are all in-house. That matters for support: when a message is blocked, the log entry and the score are visible in the customer portal rather than hidden inside a third-party filter.

The service handles the tedious parts of outbound deliverability on your behalf: rDNS on the sending IPs, feedback-loop registration with the large mailbox providers, bounce parsing, and SRS rewriting for forwarded mail. Inbound mail is not part of the product. Your MX records, Dovecot and inbound spam filtering stay exactly where they are.

The account costs $1 per month, which includes roughly 5,000 messages. Beyond that, sending is billed at $0.20 per 1,000 messages. There is no per-server or per-domain fee, so a provider with forty cPanel servers pays for volume alone. Two billing details catch people out:

Ordering is done through the InterServer customer portal at my.interserver.net, where you also find the SMTP username (mbXXXXX style, with no domain part), the password, the logs and the block management tools.

The hard ceiling is 6,000 messages per hour per sender address. Excess messages are discarded rather than queued, so a newsletter script that pushes 10,000 messages from one address in a burst loses 4,000 of them silently at the relay. Throttle at the source: cPanel’s per-domain hourly limits, Postfix’s default_destination_rate_delay, or the sending application’s own batch settings. We set the server-side cap below 6,000 so that the relay limit is never the one that fires.

New accounts are treated more strictly during a warm-up period. Expect a lower effective threshold and more aggressive scoring for the first days of sending. Migrating a busy server onto a brand-new account on a Friday evening is a bad idea; move a low-volume server first.

Logs are retained for 60 days and record the sender, recipient, subject and spam score. Message bodies are removed once delivery completes, so MailBaby cannot recover the content of a message for you later.

STARTTLS is required on the inbound connection from your server. Plain-text authentication on port 25 without TLS is refused, which is why every configuration example on this site sets hosts_require_tls or smtp_tls_security_level to at least opportunistic TLS.

The acceptable use policy excludes several categories regardless of how well the mail is formatted: cold or unsolicited outreach, gambling, cryptocurrency promotion, pharmaceutical sales and debt collection. These are not scored on merit; a customer in one of these verticals will be blocked and can get your account suspended. Screen new signups against this list before you route their mail through the relay, and route legitimate high-risk senders through a dedicated IP elsewhere.

A typical deployment keeps the panel’s mail stack unchanged and adds a smarthost route. On cPanel that is an Exim authenticator, a manualroute router and an SMTP transport with hosts_require_auth, described in our cPanel/WHM setup guide. DirectAdmin uses the exim.*.pre.conf and exim.*.post.conf override files, covered in the DirectAdmin guide. Postfix servers need a relayhost plus SASL credentials, covered in the Postfix guide.

DNS needs one change per customer domain: either add include:spf-c.mailbaby.net to the SPF record or publish a _mailbaby. verification TXT record. The two methods are compared in the SPF and verification guide. DKIM signatures generated on your server pass through untouched, which is the arrangement you want; without them MailBaby signs with its own domain and recipients see “via mailbaby.net”.

Send a test from a mailbox on the server to an external address and read the headers of the received message. The Received: chain should show your server handing off to relay.mailbaby.net, and Authentication-Results should report spf=pass, dkim=pass for your domain and, if you publish a policy, dmarc=pass. In the InterServer portal, confirm the message appears in the log with a low score. Then watch the log for the first 24 hours: a sudden run of rSPAM rejections from one address is the earliest sign of a compromised customer account, and the relay will show it before your local Exim log does.

What is MailBaby: the questions this guide answers.

Covers: What the service actually does, Pricing and what gets billed, Limits you must design around and Prohibited content.

Official documentation: RFC 5321 (SMTP), Linux man pages.

Related guides: Newsletters and mailing lists through MailBaby: staying under the 6,000/hour limit and out of spam folders · MailBaby DKIM transport signing explained: why some mail shows “via mailbaby.net” · Sending WordPress email through MailBaby: SMTP plugin setup and testing.

Updating a chat summary after every reply made my 4-turn chats 84% more expensive

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

You've built a chatbot, and you've learned that chat APIs have no memory: every turn you send the system prompt and the whole conversation again, so long chats get expensive fast. The usual fix is a running summary. Instead of the full history, you send a short summary of what was said so far.

The obvious way to build it is to update the summary after every reply. It's simple and the summary is always fresh. On short chats it's also the most expensive option, because the summary is text the model writes, and written text is billed at a much higher price.

A token is a chunk of text the model reads or writes; APIs bill per million of them. Input tokens are what you send, output tokens are what the model writes back, and output usually costs several times more. A list price is the vendor's official price per million tokens, before any discount. A turn is one user message plus one assistant reply. A summarizer call is a separate API request whose only job is to rewrite the conversation into a shorter text.

I modeled a support chat with round numbers: a 400-token system prompt, 60-token user messages, 250-token replies and a 250-token summary (about 200 words). The summarizer instruction, counted with tiktoken (o200k_base), is 83 tokens. Prices are list prices from the catalog snapshot of the LLM gateway I work on: gpt-5.6 at $2 per million input tokens and $12 per million output.

I compared three strategies for the same conversation:

Total cost of one conversation on gpt-5.6, chat and summarizer on the same model:

The per-reply summary only becomes cheaper than doing nothing at turn 19. Most support chats are over long before that.

The reason is the output price. Each per-reply summarizer call reads 643 tokens and writes 250. Those 250 output tokens cost as much as 1,500 input tokens, so 70% of the call's price is the summary text itself. The whole call costs $0.0043, more than the very first chat turn ($0.0039). On a short chat you pay that every turn to avoid resending a history that was still small.

The batched version has the same per-call problem, but it pays it rarely and only when it buys something. Its first summarizer call costs $0.0081: it reads eight turns (2,480 tokens) and replaces them with 250. From then on every turn sends about 2,230 fewer input tokens, which saves $0.0045 per turn. The call has paid for itself after two more turns: the conversation is 5% more expensive at turn 11, 1% cheaper at turn 12, and 19% cheaper at turn 20. A chat that ends before turn 11 never triggers it and costs exactly what full history costs.

Moving the summary to a cheaper model shifts both curves. With gpt-5.6-mini ($0.20 / $1.20) writing summaries for a gpt-5.6 chat, the per-reply version is still 7% more expensive at four turns but cheaper from turn 6, and the batched one is cheaper from the first summary. The batched version on claude-sonnet-5.5 with claude-haiku-5.5 as the summarizer is also cheaper from its first summary.

Check the size before each turn and summarize only when the history has grown:

Running it prints 7 8 earlier turns about a refund: the system prompt, the summary, two recent turns (four messages) and the new message. Ten turns is a starting point. A count of tokens works better than a count of turns if your messages vary a lot in length, and the usage field of each response tells you the real input size.

A summary loses detail. The model may drop an order number or a setting the user mentioned on turn 3, and with the batched version that loss happens in one jump, not gradually. I didn't measure answer quality, only price. If your bot must quote exact details from early in the chat, test summaries on real conversations first, or keep key facts in a separate structured field instead.

Prompt caching changes the math. Many vendors bill a repeated prefix at a discount. Full history is a prefix that only grows, so it caches well, while every new summary changes the prefix and breaks the cache. The catalog I used has no cache prices, so none of this is in the table.

Finally, all token sizes here are assumptions, and the break-even points move with them. Longer replies make summarizing pay off sooner; a summary close to the size of the turns it replaces never pays off. Plug your own numbers from usage into the same comparison before you pick a threshold.

It probably DOESN'T work for the reason people thought.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

We love a good optical illusion, and we've seen plenty of mind-benders over the years, but there's one really famous one that continues to intrigue scientists. The Müller-Lyer illusion looks so simple: two lines, one with arrows pointing inwards from the ends and one with arrows pointing out. One looks longer than the other, right?

A lot of people see it that way, but some people don't. Some people can immediately tell that the two horizontal lines are actually the same length (see below if you don't believe me). But the reason for this difference in perfection continues to be a topic of big debate.

This optical illusion was named after Franz Carl Müller-Lyer, its creator. Many people initially assume that the line with the arrows pointing inwards is longer than the other. But then research published in the 1960s claimed that people in African and Southeast Asian countries weren't so often fooled by the illusion. It seemed that WEIRD people were mode susceptible – that is, people whose backgrounds were Western, Educated, Industrialised, Rich and from a Democracy.

That led to a theory that the apparent difference might have something to do with people in industrialised societies being more exposed to sharp angles and straight lines when growing up, and that this somehow moulds their perception.

The problem is that the suggested difference in perception has now been called into question. As reported by Sciencing, Dorsa Amir and Chaz Firestone, anthropologists at Duke and John Hopkins, have gathered evidence opposing the theory developed in the 1960s and published their findings in the Psychological Review.

They note that many variation of the Müller-Lyer illusion have been developed, including ones that work in a similar way despite using curved lines instead of straight ones. Other similar illusions abound, including the Ebbinghaus illusion, in which two identical dots are perceived to be different sizes depending on whether they're surrounded by six large dots or eight small dots. In that case, there are no lines or angles at all.

Amir and Firestone also turned up flaws in the case studies from the 1960s, and found that later reports had misconstrued the original findings. The evidence that Africans and Asians aren't fooled by the Müller-Lyer illusion appeared to be very scant.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

But one discovery would seem to be even more conclusive in disproving the old WEIRD theory. The optical illusion has been tested on people who were born blind and later had lens replacement surgery, and it appears to trick people immediately after they gain sight despite them not having grown up seeing lines and angles around them.

With the WEIRD theory ruled out, it seems then that this old optical illusion has become weirder, becoming a mystery again

There he manages a team of designers, photographers and video editors who specialise in producing visual content and design assets for the hospitality sector. He also dances Argentine tango.
