The AI Stock Slump Is Crushing Caterpillar. Here’s Why I Like the Stock More Than Ever.

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Caterpillar shares just hit a 52-week low. Now is the time to buy.

By Matthew Benjamin – Nov 25, 2026 at 6:50AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Caterpillar ›

Shares of heavy equipment manufacturer Caterpillar (CAT -1.17%) hit a fresh 52-week low last week.

CAT shares are now down more than 9% this year, and about 51% off the high they set back in late June. That's quite a slide.

And it's why I like this stock more than ever.

There's a straightforward reason for Caterpillar's share price decline: The company is no longer considered a construction or agriculture stock. Instead, it is clearly seen by investors as an artificial intelligence stock, as I wrote back in October.

Caterpillar's big growth engine this year has been its power equipment sales, which are in high demand from AI data centers now being built across the country.

As such, Caterpillar's share price has declined amid the unwinding of the AI data center trade in recent months. Plus, despite a solid third quarter, investors were disappointed that management didn't increase its full-year revenue guidance.

Yet, despite investor jitters about the AI infrastructure build-out, I see it continuing well into 2027 and beyond. Even better for Caterpillar, electricity demand from power-hungry AI data centers is forecast to quadruple over the next decade. And 2027 could be a banner year, as AI capital expenditures are expected to shift from semiconductors and software to physical infrastructure.

Of all the resource constraints AI infrastructure faces, power may be the most pressing. The power generation systems Caterpillar manufactures will be central to solving that.

So, with CAT shares at half their price from just a few months ago, I believe now is the time to take a stake in the company.

Japan’s SBI, SMFG launch Solana venture for stablecoins, tokenization

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

SBI Solana Global plans to develop stablecoin payment infrastructure, explore AI agent micropayments and tokenize financial assets.

Japanese financial giants SBI Holdings and Sumitomo Mitsui Financial Group (SMFG) have launched launched a joint venture with the Solana Foundation to tokenize assets, develop stablecoin payment infrastructure and expand onchain financial services across Asia.

The three parties said Wednesday they had finalized agreements to transition SBI R3 Japan to new joint venture structure, with the company beginning full-scale operations as SBI Solana Global.

The company said it will focus on financial asset tokenization, real-world asset tokenization, stablecoin payments and onchain asset management.

Its plans include developing domestic and cross-border remittance infrastructure, exploring micropayments conducted by artificial intelligence agents and supporting the issuance and distribution of tokenized bonds, investment funds and loan receivables.

SBI has already expanded into both dollar- and yen-denominated stablecoins. Its crypto subsidiary SBI VC Trade began offering USDC trading in March 2025, while the group and Startale launched the yen-backed JPYSC in June 2026.

The launch follows a strategic partnership between SBI Holdings and the Solana Foundation announced in July, which outlined plans to rename SBI R3 Japan and develop financial infrastructure supporting institutional blockchain adoption.

FDA clears DeepHealth’s AI that predicts breast cancer risk 5 years ahead

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Mammo Risk was trained on 220,000 studies and tested on more than 65,000 US screening mammograms. It flags women at higher future risk rather than looking for a tumour.

The US Food and Drug Administration has cleared DeepHealth’s Mammo Risk. The AI software estimates a woman’s chance of developing breast cancer within three, four or five years. It works from a routine 3D mammogram, with no extra scan. The FDA cleared it on 23 November, the RadNet subsidiary said in a press release on Tuesday.

DeepHealth calls it the first FDA-cleared tool that predicts breast cancer risk from 3D mammogram images. It also covers combined 3D and 2D workflows. It is now on sale to healthcare providers across the US and works with systems from several vendors. 3D mammography is available at more than 90% of US mammography facilities, the company said, citing FDA figures.

Mammo Risk does not look for an existing tumour. It is meant to flag women whose scans show no signs of cancer. Some of them may still face a higher risk within five years. Providers can then adjust their screening, for example by offering extra imaging. DeepHealth sells a separate detection product, Mammo Dx.

American Cancer Society figures cited in the release put this year’s US cases of invasive breast cancer at about 321,910. Traditional risk models rely on long patient questionnaires and clinical information. Mammo Risk instead uses AI trained on 220,000 studies to read the risk from the mammogram itself.

“By launching the world’s first and only image-based risk assessment software for 3D mammography, we are moving beyond just detecting cancer towards enabling preventive pathways,” said Niccolò Stefani, business and product leader for clinical AI at DeepHealth.

An independent multicentre study covered more than 65,000 screening mammograms at 10 US clinical sites. Mammo Risk showed “strong predictive performance” in it, DeepHealth said. Results were consistent across subgroups, including Black women and women with dense breasts. Both groups have historically had poorer outcomes. Traditional models have also been less accurate for them, the company said. The release cites the study to the FDA clearance file, K262709, and gives no accuracy figures.

The software builds on an earlier DeepHealth model that predicts risk up to two years ahead. That model is CE-marked and on sale in Europe. Karolinska Institutet researchers led a 2022 study of it in Science Translational Medicine. It drew on more than 154,000 US women screened with 3D mammography. For the 12% of women at highest risk, the model showed about 2.4 times the sensitivity of the traditional Tyrer-Cuzick model.

“Everyone’s biology and breast cancer risk are different, yet too often we take a one-size-fits-all approach to screening and care. Better approaches have been long overdue,” said Victoria Smart, senior vice president of mission at the breast cancer charity Susan G. Komen.

The American Medical Association has set up a Category III billing code for image-based breast cancer risk tools. It takes effect on 1 January 2027. The code tracks how often such tools are used, DeepHealth said. It also gathers evidence that may inform future payment decisions.

Mammo Risk will join RadNet’s own screening programmes in the coming months. DeepHealth will show it at the RSNA radiology meeting in Chicago, from 29 November to 2 December. A talk on 2 December will present a study of its image-only risk model, according to an earlier DeepHealth release.

In Europe, Berlin’s Vara won a CE mark in September for AI that clears normal mammograms without a radiologist. French startup Raidium’s AI radiology viewer is in use at Moffitt Cancer Center. Mass General Brigham spinout Aevius raised $22m for clinical AI this week.

Il software che fa volare il Monza: Gustavo Varela pescato tra 60mila, ma non è l'unico

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Varela non è stato un colpo di fortuna per il Monza, che usa un sistema codificato per scovare talenti da valorizzare: lo rende noto Repubblica, che ha indagato il metodo di reclutamento del club brianzolo, che fa largo uso di algoritmi. Alla base c’è un software, sviluppato dall’ad Baldissoni e dal suo gruppo di lavoro – di cui faceva parte Francesco Vallone, oggi coordinatore dell’area tecnica in Brianza – ai tempi della Roma. Hanno iniziato a metterlo a punto nel 2011, poi lo hanno affinato e migliorato.

Oggi la piattaforma monitora in tempo reale oltre 60mila calciatori di tutto il mondo, guardando sia alle serie principali sia a quelle minori. Non mette insieme solo statistiche: valuta e combina i dati atletici dei giocatori e le loro qualità. Un esempio: evidenzia il tipo di giocata che viene fatta in un determinato contesto e zona di campo, la capacità di incidere nei momenti delicati della partita (ha un peso diverso segnare l’1-0 o il 4-0). Somma una serie di dati e permette pure la comparazione con altri profili.

Negli ultimi cinquant’anni, solo Montella e Milito avevano raggiunto la doppia cifra così rapidamente all’esordio in Serie A. Pensare che nell’ultimo campionato portoghese, con il Gil Vicente, aveva segnato appena 6 gol. Lui figlio d’arte, cresciuto tra Sporting, Paok e Benfica, che per due volte ha sfidato in campo il papà Fernando difensore. Il Monza lo ha preso dal Benfica per appena 2 milioni più 1,5 di bonus e il 20% della futura rivendita. Il club di Lisbona si è riservato il diritto di pareggiare eventuali offerte.

How The Best Manufacturers Use AI To Turn Everyday Work Into Measurable Gains

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

​Transformative technologies tend to follow a familiar arc: Rapid adoption causes growing pains, with early winners capturing outsized gains while others struggle to turn access into value. The gap narrows for some adopters as best practices mature, while others start to doubt the ROI when they don’t see results in the short term. The difference between these outcomes usually depends on two things: the willingness to prioritize practical (and often less flashy) use cases and the underlying system data in place to support AI.

We are seeing this tension play out in manufacturing with the rise of AI and model context protocol (MCP). By offering a way to interact with business systems in everyday tools like ChatGPT or Claude, MCP addresses a problem manufacturers have lived with for years: getting accurate product data to employees outside of engineering teams.

A survey my company commissioned through Talker Research of 400 management-level professionals across discrete manufacturing industries in high tech, medical devices and industrial equipment confirms widespread interest in this benefit, with 73% rating MCP as critical or very important to their AI strategy over the next 24 months and 91% reporting that interest in MCP has surged within their organizations in the past year. ​

Our research shows that interest in MCP has surged over the past year, but the approach to the next step is key—turning that interest into a measured, thoughtful implementation instead of what amounts to AI theater. ​

How to extract the most value from AI comes up in nearly every executive meeting I attend. Leaders want better AI results but also face pressure to show their boards something that makes a splash. That creates a tension: Pursue a high-visibility use case, or tackle the routine work that slows teams down every day. Choosing the routine problem is usually the fastest way to generate value.

The goal shouldn’t be to replace a 75-step workflow with an automated AI process. The more positive result usually comes from reducing that 75-step workflow to 10 well-groomed steps. Redundant handoffs are eliminated, approvals that require human judgment stay and the whole process moves quicker. This approach can be delivered faster, will likely be more reliable and will deliver real benefits.

Here’s what it looks like when you have AI take on too much too fast. If you use your MCP-enabled AI to design a new product or subassembly from scratch, you may get something that passes as a product after hours of prompts and exhaustive iterations. But you won’t know if replacement parts are available, whether any component or supplier is at risk or whether the design meets regulatory requirements. The prompt could burn hours of expensive computation, only to produce something your engineering team can’t use.

This doesn’t just cost effort. The more you ask an AI agent to do, the more expensive it gets and the harder it becomes to trust the answer. Think of it like asking a new employee to compile a report by checking 10 different systems with no manager review. The more handoffs without a checkpoint, the more likely something quietly goes wrong and the more work needs to be redone once the error is found. AI works the same way. The use cases that earn their budget are the ones scoped tightly enough to return a clear, verifiable answer.

Ask this instead: “What are the risk factors associated with these five key components I’m sourcing for a new gear assembly design, given our current supplier qualification status and open quality issues?” That question draws on records and data that live in your systems and reflects how your business truly operates. If the underlying data being queried is accurate, an engineer can get the right answer in seconds instead of spending a half day chasing it across systems. That engineer now has more time to spend on higher-value tasks, like reviewing additional designs. That’s a use case for business leaders that earns its budget.

The best prioritization will still cost undue time and energy if your system architecture and data aren’t ready for AI. MCP connects and coordinates actions across enabled applications, but the technology is only as useful as the underlying data.​

Of the C-suite executives surveyed, 89% said connected data is essential to realizing AI’s full value. Conversely, nine in 10 manufacturing executives concluded that AI running on disconnected systems isn’t a capability; it’s a liability.​

​MCP can connect disparate systems, but those connections only help if the underlying data agrees. When systems contain conflicting or outdated records, AI cannot reliably determine which version to trust. Consistent data across systems removes that ambiguity. AI benefits from metadata, which provides context by defining what each field means, how it’s structured and how the business uses it, so AI knows how to interpret the values in those fields.

When an engineer asks about component risk, the answer requires data from product lifecycle management (PLM), quality management (QMS) and enterprise resource planning (ERP). If the data in those systems is missing or contradictory, how will the AI determine which is correct? The results will be more precise if the data is consistent across all three systems and metadata indicates which fields are related to component and supplier risk.

That’s why I see MCP as an immediate productivity driver for manufacturers with strong data hygiene. When data is accurate and structured, MCP can significantly reduce the complexity of connecting systems.

Within three years, survey respondents expect connected AI agents to influence or automate almost one-third of their daily business processes, and over three-quarters anticipate significant or transformational business impact in that window.​

Manufacturers that create lasting value from their AI investments will not chase the most visible use cases first. They will build on connected, trusted product data, apply MCP to focused daily work and scale from results their teams can verify.

History shows new technologies create early winners, but lasting value comes from turning access into practical results.

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

The surprising — and fun — way brands are AI-proofing themselves

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Play is becoming the route to making truly creative, human work. As AI makes polished output cheap, abundant, and instantaneous, brands are increasingly looking to offer expressions of character, and ways to stand apart from the inevitable homogenization of content and communications.

Externally, that impulse is showing up everywhere. Brands are lifting the lid on the creative process through behind-the-scenes content, embracing deliberately handmade visual expression and even using “No AI” disclaimers, like recent campaigns from Apple or Aerie.

Each is an attempt to signal the presence of human thought and distinguish meaningful creative work from the flood of so-called slop.

Internally too, creativity is the priority. A Marketing Week study in 2025 found that almost two-thirds of brand marketers had increased their focus on creative quality, while 63.9% rated it among the more influential factors in marketing effectiveness.

Yet striving for more creativity is not the same as knowing how to enable it. Brand managers are often asked to protect consistency, reduce risk, and deliver at speed. Briefs are often loaded with history, data, and preconceptions; teams are rewarded for control; often, ideas are filtered out before they have room to develop. And the idea of true creativity can be abstract, intimidating, and unattainable.

Play might sound unserious in that environment. But in fact, it can be a working method, helpful and inspiring; permission to experiment and make unexpected connections, combined with the expertise to select and execute the right idea.

Unlocking its commercial advantage requires leaders to rethink how they brief teams, frame creativity, and manage brands. Here’s how to get started.

Make room to play

Original thinking needs guardrails, but also room to grow. A strong brief should get teams to the point where they can begin exploring, not overwhelm them with years of history and layers of data. The best chief marketing officers or brand managers get it. They do the research in advance, then set out the ambition for the brand with a suggested set of parameters and say “go and play’”(as Chris Willingham, CMO at Brompton Bicycle, did in our recent work together), offering clarity and trust to create the conditions for better work.

This also makes the notion of creativity less obscure or intimidating. “Get creative” can feel like a loose and impossibly lofty instruction. “Go and play” is an invitation to explore. It shifts the focus from finding the right answer immediately to following ideas and seeing where they lead. The wrong idea can still reveal the right solution; rejected routes also clarify what a brand is not. Play demystifies creativity without lowering the standard. One time, a workwear manufacturer client challenged us to create something that looked deliberately “not right.” It was the perfect invitation to play, and resulted in a design that felt structured and familiar but punctuated by unusual interactions.

Play to consumer intelligence

Alongside a change in language needs to come an appreciation of a changing consumer and their needs. Brand consistency once offered reassurance. But today, the consumer expects their brands to be more animated, dynamic, nimble and responsive to the times we’re living in.   

Like people, brands can—and should—have different facets. Reliable in one moment, spontaneous or surprising in another. Wise, the money transfer platform, is a great example of a brand getting that balance right; the experience feels trustworthy, but also conversational, quirky, and human. Interactions with Wise are like talking to my intelligent, funny friend. It is an experience miles ahead of what banks like Barclays offer.

It also shows how important the notion of play is in the expressions of a brand—visual, verbal, behavioral. A brand doesn’t need to be irreverent or rebellious to embrace play. You can be one of the most serious companies but still allow playful elements of your brand to convey trust, accessibility or a more human approach. The connectivity provider rSIM, for example, offers a serious B2B product to healthcare, banking, and other utilities. But its brand and articulation are full of vim and vigor. It turns up with a unique character in a very technical field.

Embrace the unexpected

A more playful mindset also allows you to make unexpected connections, to produce the leap that makes an idea distinctive. Take the International Olympic Committee’s multiyear licensing agreement with Sonic the Hedgehog. Is it consistent with the famously strict Olympic brand guidelines? Debatable. But would Pierre de Coubertin, founder of the modern Olympic Games, approve? Absolutely. It’s a contemporary articulation of the Olympic ideal, which is to bring the youth of the world together in peaceful competition—and Sonic is a way to connect with young people around the world.  

Strategic play can uncover those connections, giving established purpose a fresh and culturally relevant expression. But it will always go hand in hand with judgment. The point is not about endless play or ideation. The commercial advantage comes from combining freedom with craft, self-editing, and the confidence to stop when the right idea appears.

Exclusive: The Rings of Power season 3 episode 7 director breaks down the Balrog sequence

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Simon Pegg voices the Balrog in The Rings of Power season 3 episode 7, bringing the iconic creature to life. His climactic scene comes opposite Sauron, who comes to ask him the secrets of forging the One Ring in the fires of Mount Doom and surviving.

Pegg actually came on set to film the sequence with Charlie Vickers, according to the episode's director Stefan Schwartz, which added another layer to the sequence. Despite being told they wouldn't get to his bit until after lunch, Schwartz says Pegg turned up in the morning, and during every break in filming they'd speak through the history of the Balrog.

Then during the sequence with Sauron, Pegg was present on set with a microphone, speaking the Balrog's lines. "Charlie had to look at this big light on the crane that was moving around where the Balrog was going to go," Schwartz explains. "So Charlie was looking there, but Simon was actually working with him, and we could rehearse it and play it in different ways."

He adds: "It was really nice to have him there on set, and all of it was played opposite him. And in fact, towards the end, for their tête-à-tête, Simon was right there, and it really felt they were definitely acting with each other rather than in a vacuum, which could so easily happen."

Bringing the Balrog visually to the screen was another challenge for the director too. "The tricky thing with the Balrog was we don't ever see the Balrog very much in anything before, and it needs to express emotion and anger and coolness," he explains.

"We worked out that the Balrog would have these different colors. It would be a cooler color, then it would warm up as it got angry or as it was getting emotional, and then start really flaming when it was angry."

The final result is impressive, and the flaming monster is a real highlight of episode 7. For more of a deep dive into the iconic crafting of the One Ring, check out what the director had to say about the "pressure" of bringing it to the screen.

Ryanair Delays Free Seat Assignments, Says Passengers Who Don’t Pay “Clearly Don’t Mind Where They Sit”

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ryanair passengers who decline to pay for seat selection must now wait until only a few hours before departure to learn where they will sit. The airline says the change preserves more choice for customers willing to pay, which makes commercial sense. It also creates another incentive to purchase a seat simply to end the uncertainty.

Ryanair has changed when it assigns complimentary random seats to passengers who do not purchase one.

Previously, passengers generally received their seat assignment when checking in up to 24 hours before departure. Under the new approach, the seat may not appear on the digital boarding pass until approximately three to four hours before the flight.

The change was introduced with Ryanair’s winter schedule at the end of October.

Passengers can still check in beginning 72 hours before departure, but those who decline to pay for a seat may have to wait until the day of travel for the airline to update their boarding pass with an actual seat assignment.

Ryanair told RTE that it received complaints from passengers who wanted to purchase a particular seat on the day of departure, only to find that desirable seats had already been distributed through free random allocation.

Its explanation included a very Ryanair-like observation:

“Passengers who choose a free of charge random seat clearly don’t mind where they sit.”

Therefore, Ryanair now keeps more seats available for purchase until closer to departure. Passengers who have selected random seating will have their digital boarding passes automatically updated once the airline assigns them a seat.

The logic is understandable: if someone is willing to pay for an aisle or window seat three hours before departure, Ryanair would rather sell it than discover it gave that seat away for free the previous evening. But let’s not pretend that preserving customer choice is the only objective here…those who are going to buy a seat usually do so in advance, so we are only talking about last-minute purchases.

I have no problem with Ryanair charging for advance seat assignments. That is the low-cost carrier “unbundled” model: advertise a very inexpensive base fare and allow passengers to decide which extras are worth purchasing.

I have flown Ryanair many times and approach it with those expectations. If I care where I sit, I pay. If I do not, I take whatever the airline gives me (of course hoping I end up with a window or aisle seat).

But the timing of this change creates pressure that did not exist before. A passenger can complete check-in yet remain uncertain about the seat assignment until shortly before leaving for the airport. Some will become impatient or anxious and pay simply to resolve it.

That is certainly good ancillary-revenue strategy. It is also another example of Ryanair understanding exactly how to monetize uncertainty. The airline knows that passengers become increasingly willing to spend as departure approaches. A middle seat may be tolerable when the trip is weeks away, but somehow looks much less appealing on the morning of the flight.

Ryanair’s family seating policy remains important here.

Under the airline’s published policy, children between two and 11 years old must be seated beside at least one accompanying adult. The adult is required to reserve a seat, while up to four children receive adjacent reserved seats without an additional seat-selection charge. Additional adults and passengers aged 12 or older who do not purchase seats may be assigned elsewhere.

Families should be aware that they are not guaranteed to be seated next to eachother (and it would not surprise me if the Ryanair algorithm specially avoids this in order to encourage famlies to pre-pay for seats on the next trip).

Ryanair now waits until approximately three to four hours before departure to assign complimentary random seats, allowing it to preserve more desirable inventory for passengers willing to pay.

The commercial reasoning is clear, and I do not fault Ryanair for selling seat assignments. But delaying the free assignment also creates anxiety that will persuade some passengers to purchase a seat they otherwise would not have bought. It is clever revenue management, but hardly an improvement for passengers.

they fired the CEO with the same confidence you say "never flying Ryanair again" https://t.co/s63NRElFlI

Pakistan’s Zeeshan Ali is the first foreign astronaut on China’s station

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

He trained for seven months in Beijing, much of it on the language. He will spend up to 10 days on Tiangong running experiments before flying home on Shenzhou-23.

The Shenzhou-23 and Shenzhou-24 crews aboard Tiangong, with Pakistan’s Muhammad Zeeshan Ali at the back on the right

The Shenzhou-24 crew entered China’s Tiangong space station at 2:35pm Beijing time on Wednesday. It had lifted off about six hours earlier from the Jiuquan Satellite Launch Center in north-west China. On board was Muhammad Zeeshan Ali, a 40-year-old Pakistani pilot. He is the first foreign astronaut to fly with China’s crewed space programme.

Ali flew with commander Tang Hongbo, 51, and flight engineer Wu Yi, 39, on a Long March-2F rocket. Andy Wong and Kanis Leung reported the launch for the Associated Press, and Shi Bu and Alessandro Diviggiano for Reuters. The joint flight is part of an agreement China and Pakistan signed in February 2025.

Ali is the first Pakistani in orbit, according to Reuters. He is flying as a payload specialist and will stay at the station for five to 10 days. He will then return to Earth aboard the Shenzhou-23 spacecraft, China’s official Xinhua news agency reported.

Ali trained for seven months in Beijing after he was chosen in April. The hardest part was the language, he said in an official Chinese video cited by the Financial Times.

“The real challenge, honestly speaking, was mastering the Chinese language for all the switches, all the software, all the programmes, all the voice announcements,” Ali said.

Tang told reporters on Tuesday that differences in language, culture and psychology had made working together harder. Both sides’ efforts were paying off, he said, according to AP. It is Tang’s third mission, and he is the first astronaut to board Tiangong three times, state broadcaster CCTV said.

The crew will run more than 90 experiments and technology tests, according to Xinhua. Ali’s include a study of photosynthetic cyanobacteria, a type of bacteria that makes energy from light. He will also help test carbon fibre materials that repair small cracks in spacecraft on their own, Reuters reported.

Pakistani Prime Minister Shehbaz Sharif marked the launch in a post on X.

“This is not just one astronaut’s journey, but the journey of 240 million Pakistanis,” Sharif wrote.

China’s President Xi Jinping and Pakistan’s President Asif Ali Zardari exchanged letters of congratulation. CCTV reported that Xi called the station a “boat” for peace, friendship and cooperation.

Ahsan Iqbal, Pakistan’s planning minister, said at the launch centre that he hoped for more cooperation, including a joint lunar mission. Asked whether Pakistan could one day send its own astronauts, he said: “Certainly that day will come.”

Space ties between the two countries go back to the early 1990s. China has launched Pakistani satellites, and a Pakistani-built rover is due to fly on a Chinese lunar mission around 2028, Reuters reported. In September, Greece also picked its first astronaut, for a flight in 2027.

Tiangong has been fully operational since late 2022. China is promoting it as a base for international cooperation, while the NASA-led International Space Station faces possible retirement by 2030, Reuters reported.

The handover is also unusual. Shenzhou-23 commander Zhu Yangzhu will stay on board for a full year to study how the body copes with long missions. That will be the longest single stay in orbit by a Chinese astronaut.

China plans to land its first crew on the Moon by 2030, despite this year’s delay to its Chang’e-7 lunar mission. NASA is aiming to land astronauts again in 2028.

Windows Loudness Equalization, reverse-engineered and ported to PipeWire

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Windows Loudness Equalization, reverse-engineered and ported to PipeWire

Windows' Loudness Equalization for PipeWire, reverse engineered from WMALFXGFXDSP.dll. It evens out the volume of each application, so quiet and loud parts play at a similar level.

Requires PipeWire ≥ 1.4, and GTK 3 for the window.

To install only one part, use make -C modules/loudness, make -C modules/limiter or make -C app. To uninstall, run sudo make uninstall and restart the same services.

Open Loudness Equalization from your application menu, or run pw-loudness-eq.

The settings live in a PipeWire metadata object, so they can also be scripted:

Changes apply immediately and are kept across restarts.

Defaults are in /usr/share/pipewire/pipewire.conf.d/60-loudness-eq.conf. To override them, copy it to ~/.config/pipewire/pipewire.conf.d/.

Settings changed at runtime are saved in ~/.local/state/pw-loudness-eq/state.conf and take precedence over this file.

The loudness module sets audioconvert.filter-graph.90 on each app's playback stream, so the plugin runs inside that stream, before its volume and before mixing, like a Windows stream effect.

The limiter module does what Windows' audio engine does on every playback device: after the mix and the software volume, it runs the output through a look-ahead limiter. It's always on, independent of Loudness Equalization, as on Windows.

Not processed: JACK clients, streams with more than 8 channels, and Flatpak apps that use PipeWire directly (those going through PulseAudio are processed).

A broadband automatic gain control. Despite the name, it doesn't equalize the spectrum.

The boost can push pure tones such as a whistle well above full scale; the limiter catches that.

Ported from CAudioLimiter in AudioEng.dll. audiodg.exe puts it on every render device, after its effects, before the device.

On Windows the device's software volume comes before the limiter; in PipeWire it comes after the device's filter graphs. So the plugin gets the volume of each channel (kept in sync by the module), measures peaks on the signal as it will be after the volume, and applies only its gain. Once PipeWire applies the volume, the result is the same as Windows'. Hardware volume comes after the limiter on both.

Windows Loudness Equalization, reverse-engineered and ported to PipeWire

Lincoln sack head coach duo Cohen and Shaw

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Chris Cohen (left) and Tom Shaw had been on the coaching staff together at Lincoln for a number of years before they got the top job together

Lincoln City have sacked their head coaches Chris Cohen and Tom Shaw just six months after they took joint charge of the Championship side.

The pair departed a little over 12 hours after they oversaw a 4-0 defeat at fellow strugglers Charlton Athletic on Tuesday.

The loss leaves the Imps just three points above the relegation places in what is the club's first season back in the second tier for 65 years.

Cohen and Shaw were assistant coaches under Michael Skubala when Lincoln were promoted as League One title winners last season.

They were surprisingly promoted to replace their boss as a managerial double act.

What next for Lincoln City after coach duo exit?

The duo oversaw just four wins from 17 league games this season, and Tuesday's loss at The Valley was their fifth in succession and heaviest since being thumped 6-0 by West Ham in mid-October.

Lincoln's chairman Ron Fowler, the American billionaire who took a controlling stake in the club when they were on their way to promotion last season, said the decision to sack the pair was in the "best interests" of the Imps.

"We have worked incredibly hard to reach the Championship and will do everything in our power to retain our status in this division," he told the club website., external

First-team coach Shaun Pearson will take charge of Lincoln, with the support of Paul McShane and David Preece, on an interim basis.

Lincoln are next in action on Saturday when they host high-flying Wolves.

When Cohen and Shaw got the job at Sincil Bank it came somewhat as a shock because managing football teams in tandem is somewhat of a curiosity in the game.

Liverpool famously did with Gerard Houllier and Roy Evans in 1998 when the partnership lasted six months as results slumped, while Tottenham gave it a go with Doug Livermore and Ray Clemence earlier that same decade.

The hope was that the Cohen-Shaw show would be a lot more like Coventry's success story under John Sillett and George Curtis, who guided the Sky Blues to FA Cup glory in 1987.

Lincoln's decision-makers were proactive in talking up their decision, with sporting director Jez George explaining in June that the appointment "wasn't a case of do the easy thing, but it was absolutely the right thing for the club".

Shaw and Cohen were also quizzed at length about how the joint role would work, starting with "who's going to make the subs?"

Cohen was upfront when he said "from the outside looking in, it's not normal", but the former Nottingham Forest and West Ham midfielder insisted that the job share was "really normal" to them.

How peculiar it might have looked, or how the dynamics between the pair worked, all become somewhat irrelevant in recent weeks as Lincoln's dire run of form has plunged them towards the relegation places.

Pre-match, post-match and topical Imps content

Lincoln City fans always thought this season would be tough and that they'd lose more than they win, but it was the manner of the performances recently that was most concerning.

Strange team selections in the past two matches in particular, including playing left-back Adam Reach as a right winger against Charlton Athletic in their final match in charge, was always going to raise questions, especially when the results didn't go their way, and that has ultimately been the case.

The appointment of Chris Cohen and Tom Shaw was going to be seen as a risk - joint managers/head coaches very rarely work and add in the lack of managerial experience, especially at this level.

First-team coach Shaun Pearson, who only recently joined the club , now has the task to try to turn a team low on confidence, low on ideas and low on belief into one that can stop one of the promotion favourites in Wolves on Saturday lunchtime.

Whoever's next in the dugout, however, won't come in with a massive jump in expectations of a climb up the table.

Imps supporters will be more than happy to just see their side secure a second season in the Championship.

Hong Kong launches tokenized debt platform with 24-hour settlement

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The HKMA’s new platform supports settlement in multiple currencies and handles debt issuance, interest payments and repayment at maturity.

The Hong Kong Monetary Authority (HKMA) launched a platform Wednesday that supports round-the-clock settlement of tokenized debt securities in multiple currencies.

The CMU Digital Asset Platform, developed and operated by CMU OmniClear Limited on behalf of the HKMA, uses cash tokens for payment and connects with conventional payment systems and central bank digital currencies.

Its onchain delivery-versus-payment model ensures that the securities and corresponding payment transfer together, or neither transfer occurs. This means the buyer receives the securities only if the seller receives payment.

The platform also handles issuance, interest payments and repayment at maturity, and supports the tokenization of existing debt securities. The HKMA said it aims to improve settlement efficiency and reduce operational costs.

The launch follows a separate expansion of Hong Kong’s digital payment infrastructure. On Nov. 2, the HKMA upgraded its Project Ensemble pilot to support 24/7 interbank settlement of tokenized deposit transactions using tokenized central bank money.

The pilot phase launched in November 2025 and initially used Hong Kong’s conventional real-time gross settlement system. Its applications have included corporate treasury management and transactions involving tokenized money market funds.

'Smart lighting is a journey rather than a single purchase' — why I’m still investing in Philips Hue 8 years later and what I’d buy this Black Friday

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join the Tom's Guide Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Eight years ago, I bought my first Philips Hue product: the Hue Go Portable Lamp. Eight years later, the bowl-shaped, battery-powered lamp still works, and it has quite a few friends.

Philips Hue is one of the few tech ecosystems I’ve genuinely enjoyed investing in, and one that has become more valuable to me over time. But Philips Hue does have a reputation for being expensive. That's why I consider Black Friday deals one of the best opportunities to either get started with Hue or expand an existing setup.

Ahead of the shopping holiday, I asked Marco Merolla, Executive Vice President and Business Leader of Connected Lighting at Philips Hue, about the company's approach to affordability, product longevity and the future of smart lighting.

His answers shed some light on why customers like me keep coming back, and where there's still room for improvement when it comes to connected smart homes.

I'm vehemently anti-big-light. One of my proudest domestic achievements is successfully converting my fiancé to the cause, too.

My apartment is laden with lamps, most of which are from IKEA. I also have a Hue light strip running along the back of my Lovesac couch and Hue bulbs in the built-in pendant lights above my kitchen island. But generally, rather than buying smart-specific fixtures, I prefer picking out lamps that complement my mid-century modern decor and making them smart myself.

Merolla described smart lighting as a gradual progression rather than an all-at-once overhaul. Most people start with a single room or routine and build outward as they see how it fits their space. Still, I was curious how Hue reconciles its need to keep selling products with customers' expectations that their relatively expensive lights will last.

If you're just getting started with Philips Hue, this four-pack is an affordable way to turn the lamps you already own into smart lights, complete with adjustable white lighting, millions of colors and the option to expand your setup down the road.

“Our goal is not to encourage replacement for its own sake, but to give consumers meaningful reasons to expand and enhance their existing smart lighting setup as their needs change,” Merolla said.

The other side of that equation is lowering the barrier to entry without fragmenting the system. Hue's more affordable Essential range runs on the same underlying software and ecosystem connectivity as its flagship lights, trimming peak brightness and color performance to hit a friendlier price. It’s a smart option if you're outfitting an entire apartment and don't need top-tier output in every corner lamp..

I've extensively tested competing smart lighting products in varying price ranges from Govee, Wyze and Nanoleaf. Govee, in particular, makes some incredibly fun lighting fixtures, and its adoption of Matter has made it a more compelling option for Apple Home users like myself.

But I've experienced software glitches with these other brands over the years. Nearly a decade into my smart home journey, I simply don’t have patience for connected gadgets that don't work as expected 100% of the time. Sharing a home with someone less interested in technology makes reliability and simplicity even more important.

“Physical controls remain important, since not everyone wants to reach for their phone every time they adjust the lights,” Merolla said.

While you don't technically need a Bridge to get started with Hue, I'd recommend picking one up while it's on sale to unlock whole-home lighting control, advanced automations, Apple Home integration and more opportunities to expand your setup.

Despite my investment in Hue, I rarely need to open the app for everyday lighting controls. Between wall-mounted Hue dimmer switches and shortcuts I've configured in my iPhone's Control Center through Apple Home, the lights feel as immediate as traditional switches.

You don't actually need a Bridge to get started. Bluetooth-enabled Hue bulbs work directly with your phone for up to 10 lights. But upgrading to a Bridge unlocks remote access, advanced automations, Apple Home integration and entertainment synchronization, while scaling up to 50 lights and 12 accessories on the standard model.

If you're eyeing the newer Bridge Pro, you get triple the capacity, wireless placement without an Ethernet tether, and MotionAware, a feature that uses subtle signal shifts across your existing bulbs to detect movement without dedicated motion sensors.

If you're serious about building out your smart home, the Bridge Pro offers more room to expand, faster performance and MotionAware technology that can turn compatible Hue lights into motion sensors.

“The Hue Bridge continues to play an important role in delivering a robust, connected lighting experience, while innovations such as MotionAware demonstrate how lighting infrastructure can become more intelligent and responsive to people’s needs,” Merolla said.

If you're planning to expand beyond a few bulbs, I'd invest in the infrastructure now rather than revisit your setup later.

If you've already established a Hue ecosystem, the Philips Hue Play HDMI Sync Box is easily one of my favorite ways to get more out of it.

What I love about the experience is that it isn't limited to a light strip behind my TV. The lights I've already accumulated around my living space can react to what's happening on screen, making my apartment feel like one cohesive entertainment setup.

One of my absolute favorite smart home accessories, the 8K Sync Box lets you synchronize up to 10 compatible Philips Hue lights with what's happening on your TV through connected HDMI devices, making your entire living room feel like an extension of the screen.

Hue has also been finding new ways to make entertainment lighting more interesting. Earlier this year, it introduced Sports Live, which uses live soccer match data to trigger lighting effects. It doesn't even require a Sync Box if you already have a Bridge and compatible color lights.

The Sync Box is admittedly a luxury, and it wouldn't be my first Hue purchase if I were starting from scratch. But it's exactly the kind of accessory I'd wait until Black Friday to buy

As much as I appreciate Hue's reliability, I'd love to see more intelligence built into the experience. Whenever I add another lamp to my living room, for example, I have to revisit my entertainment area's lighting configuration. Smarter spatial mapping could make that process considerably easier.

The product that started my Philips Hue obsession eight years ago, this compact, bowl-shaped, battery-powered lamp is incredibly versatile for everything from setting the mood at dinner to adding a colorful accent to a bookshelf, and remains one of my favorite entry points into the Hue ecosystem.

I'm not the only one looking for refinements. In a 2026 wishlist discussion on the Philips Hue subreddit, users called for more granular brightness controls, better Natural Light scene integration with third-party platforms and the ability to group multiple bulbs in a single fixture.

Merolla told me that the company sees an opportunity to move beyond simply controlling individual devices.

“We believe the future of smart lighting is less about controlling individual devices and more about creating experiences that naturally respond to the way people live.”

If I had to rebuild my smart home tomorrow, I'd probably start exactly where I did eight years ago: with a little Hue Go or a handful of smart bulbs.

The beauty of building a smart lighting ecosystem is that you don't need to buy everything at once. And with Black Friday discounts, you can take that first step (or if you’re like me, your next one) for considerably less.

Turn a Cheap Christmas Tree Into a Showstopper – 6 Easy Steps to Make It Lush and Luxurious

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Beautiful plants and ideas to brighten your garden.

Expert tips for growing fruit, vegetables and herbs at home.

Sign up to get fresh gardening ideas, inspiration and expert advice delivered straight to your inbox.

Grow These 6 Beautiful Living Christmas Trees in Pots – Then Enjoy Them in Your Garden for Years

Grow Your Own Christmas Decorations – 7 Plants to Add to Your Garden for Festive Displays Year After Year

Michelle Obama's Twig Tree Is Perfect For The Holidays – Here's How To Make Your Own From Garden Cuttings

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

You don't need to splash out on a premium tree this Christmas. With a little know-how, you can turn a budget option into something truly special.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

When you take the kids out to choose a Christmas tree, everyone has in mind a lush, deep green, perfectly shaped evergreen that is featured in holiday advertisements. But not every evergreen in the lot has that kind of sex appeal, especially budget trees for sale in grocery stores, supermarkets, and big-box retailers. These affordable trees can look thin, sparse, and like they didn’t get any sleep last night.

But don’t worry, an inexpensive grocery store Christmas tree can be “spruced up” nicely with a few simple tricks. Yes, you can turn ugly ducklings into beautiful centerpieces by taking steps to make a Christmas tree look fuller, well-structured, and fresh. I’ll explain how to make a real Christmas tree look better with Christmas tree care tips that boost your tree's wow-power.

In this guide, I'm going to walk you through the easy steps you can take to improve your tree dramatically, from a sparse Christmas tree to a festive showstopper. And I promise it won’t cost you a fortune.

There are many reasons that a freshly cut Christmas tree can look droopy and sparse in the store. These include transport stress and dehydration. One of the quickest fixes with a big punch is to make a fresh cut on the bottom of the trunk, taking off around ½ inch. A good folding saw, such as the Rexberi 11-Inch Folding Saw from Amazon, will make light work of this, and it's handy for pruning jobs around the garden, too.

Immediately after cutting, pop the tree into a sturdy stand with water. The Krinner Genie Christmas Tree Stand has a foot-pedal mechanism that makes it easy to secure the tree, plus a built-in water reservoir. But it's not cheap, and if you're buying a budget tree, chances are you'll want a budget-friendly stand to go with it. The Leejec Christmas Tree Stand is a good, affordable alternative, with a generous water reservoir and a handy water level indicator.

Don't leave the tree sitting around after making the cut. The cut surface can dry out, reducing moisture uptake, so getting it into water promptly gives it the best chance of staying fresh.

That sorrowful evergreen you brought home needs time to relax. Once you prune off the very bottom of the trunk, set the tree in a bucket of water and give it time to rehydrate. Leave it wrapped in its netting overnight, which will also make it easier to maneuver into its stand the next day.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Once the tree is securely in place, remove the wrapping and let the branches gradually drop and spread into their natural shape for a few hours. You might be surprised how much fuller it looks after a little time to settle.

Now it's time to give your tree a little makeover. Walk around it and look carefully at its shape. There will likely be a few stray or uneven branches that need tidying up, particularly if you've bought a cheaper tree that hasn't been professionally shaped.

To prune your Christmas tree, take a sharp pair of bypass pruners – Fiskars Bypass Pruning Shears from Amazon are a good, affordable option – and start by removing any broken, damaged, or awkwardly protruding branches. Trim back overly long branches to a side shoot where possible, rather than cutting bluntly through the tips.

Pay particular attention to the lower branches. Remove any that drag on the floor or interfere with the stand, making clean cuts close to the trunk without damaging the bark. This will give the tree a neater silhouette and make it easier to reach the water reservoir.

Step back regularly to check your progress, working toward a balanced cone shape without making the tree look unnaturally perfect. Go easy, though – you can always take more off, but you can't put a branch back.

Starting at the bottom of the tree, gently separate any branches that have become compressed during transport. Work your way upward, easing crowded branches apart and encouraging them to spread into their natural positions. Take care not to force stiff branches, as they can snap.

Once you've worked your way around the tree, step back and look for any obvious gaps. You may be able to gently reposition flexible branches to make the tree look fuller, but avoid bending or twisting them into unnatural shapes.

Pay particular attention to the lower branches, spreading them outward where possible to help disguise the trunk and tree stand. Near the top, gently separate crowded branches to create a more balanced, tapered shape. Don't worry about making everything perfectly symmetrical – a little natural irregularity is part of the charm of a real Christmas tree.

Have you ever used hair extensions? Or had a friend who used hair weaving to change the volume of their hair? You can use this same technique to fill in the canopy of your Christmas tree by tucking extra evergreen branches into bare spots. It’s a quick way to give a sparse Christmas tree a fuller figure.

What to use for this type of branch extension? Start with any healthy branches you removed when pruning your tree earlier. You can also use evergreen clippings from your own yard or ask for spare branches from the Christmas tree lot. Choose branches that closely match your tree for the most natural finish, or mix two or three types of evergreen to add depth and texture.

For larger bare patches, try tucking in realistic faux evergreen sprays, like these Hananona pine branches. Cedar and pine sprays work particularly well, blending into the natural foliage while adding extra volume.

Tuck the cut ends in toward the trunk, positioning the foliage to disguise any obvious gaps. Use green floral wire, like this Pauleen wire from Amazon, to secure the stems discreetly to existing branches, making sure they're firmly attached. Step back occasionally to check the overall effect, adding a few more sprigs wherever the tree still looks sparse.

We all have good sides and bad sides, and your Christmas tree might too. If one side is lopsided or less impressive, simply turn it toward the wall. Of course, this trick works less well if you have a revolving tree stand.

Some gaps may still be visible after fluffing up the branches and adding extras, but that's where clever decorating comes to the rescue. Tuck large ornaments deep inside the tree, closer to the trunk, to disguise bare spots and create a sense of depth.

Another great trick is to cluster smaller ornaments in groups of three to make sparse areas look fuller. If one part of the tree is overloaded with decorations, move a few into the gaps to create a more balanced look. You can also use pinecones, artificial flowers, or decorative picks to add texture and interest.

Finally, try weaving wide ribbons or garlands through the branches to disguise any remaining gaps. Rather than wrapping them tightly around the outside, tuck sections deeper into the tree and let them billow outward. This creates the illusion of a much fuller, more luxurious Christmas tree without spending a fortune.

Keeping your tree looking good until Christmas requires a little effort. Here are some top tips:

Teo Spengler is a master gardener and a docent at the San Francisco Botanical Garden, where she hosts public tours. She has studied horticulture and written about nature, trees, plants and gardening for more than two decades. Before turning her focus to gardening, she worked as an attorney and legal writer, experience she also brings to her role as our Gardening Lawyer. Her extended family includes some 30 houseplants and hundreds of outdoor plants, including 250 trees, which are her main passion. Spengler currently splits her life between San Francisco and the French Basque Country, though she was raised in Alaska, giving her experience of gardening in a range of climates.

AMD's Radeon RX 9060 XT sets new GPU frequency world record of 4.837 GHz

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AMD's smaller RDNA 4 GPU gets one step closer to 5 GHz.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

AMD has set a new GPU overclocking world record by pushing the Radeon RX 9060 XT to 4,837 MHz. Veteran overclocker Bill Alverson and AMD VP Jack Huynh found the limits of this particular chip as part of a celebration of the opening of a new Micro Center location in Austin, Texas. According to the OC World Record Museum database maintained by SkatterBencher, Huynh and Alverson set the new record on November 15.

If you were expecting to see this record from AMD's high-end RX 9070 XT, you're not crazy. The record was indeed achieved using the midrange RDNA 4 part rather than AMD’s flagship GPU. The RX 9060 XT uses a smaller Navi 44 GPU featuring 2,048 shading units (32 compute units), a 160W TDP, 16GB of GDDR6 memory on a 128-bit bus, and a stock boost clock speed of up to 3,130 MHz.

A NEW @microcenter in Austin. An INCREDIBLE community. And a GPU WORLD RECORD. I had an absolute blast celebrating with Austin's gamers, PC builders, and creators. From meeting fans and snapping photos to building PCs and sharing our passion for all things tech. And then came an even BIGGER moment... I joined forces with Bill Alverson and our @AMD overclocking team. Armed with liquid nitrogen and pushing frequencies to the absolute limit, we set our sights on something BIG: breaking the GPU FREQUENCY WORLD RECORD! And we did it! 4.837 GHz! And we captured the whole thing on camera, including the moment we broke the record! Epic energy. Epic builds. WORLD RECORDS!November 24, 2026

The new frequency record was achieved using liquid nitrogen cooling. At 4,837 MHz, the GPU was running approximately 54.5% faster than its advertised boost frequency. A GPU-Z screenshot confirms the record breaking frequency with the GPU temperature at -49°C, 62W power draw, and voltage at 1.434V.

Of course, nobody should expect these numbers to bear any relationship to the clock speeds that can be achieved from any stock, air-cooled RX 9060 XT. Liquid nitrogen and liquid helium are commonly used in extreme overclocking, as they bring component temperatures well below what air and liquid cooling at ambient temperatures can achieve, essentially removing thermal limits from an overclocking attempt and enabling large amounts of power to be pushed into a chip for the highest clocks.

But these coolants require careful preparation of hardware, constant monitoring, and custom heatsinks to contain them while an extreme overclocking run is in progress, and they're only useful for short durations due to evaporation.

Such record-breaking frequencies are primarily intended to demonstrate the absolute limits of the hardware itself, rather than serve as an example of sustained performance for everyday use cases.

This isn’t the first time enthusiasts have pushed AMD’s RDNA 4 GPU to extreme frequencies. The RX 9060 XT previously reached 4,769 MHz in February 2026, followed by another attempt in June which reportedly reached 4,789 MHz. The latest attempt is 48 MHz higher compared to the previous record, bringing AMD’s latest GPU IP closer than ever to the elusive 5 GHz milestone.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

For the average gamer, the biggest obstacle to getting the most out of an RX 9060 XT isn't its overclocking headroom, but its high price in late 2026. The RX 9060 XT 16GB had an MSRP of just $349, but these cards are currently selling for over $700, making this formerly budget-friendly enthusiast card a poor value today. Achieving a world-record GPU frequency on one of these chips is an impressive achievement in extreme overclocking, but it's unlikely to translate into more Radeon gamers any time soon.

The Best TVs We’ve Tested (2026): Samsung, LG, TCL, Sony, and More.

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Buying a new TV is always a big decision, namely because its a big piece of tech with a permanent place in your home that you'll be staring at for hours on end. Not to mention, some of the best of the best cost thousands of dollars. It doesn't help that most TVs look exactly the same and have nondescript names like the Samsung S95H—not great for narrowing your options.

Fear not. After stress-testing multiple TVs this year, I can attest to one simple truth: in 2026, you have more great options than ever. Art TVs and RGB TVs exploded onto the market and made the typical “just buy an OLED TV” advice seem like an ancient relic of a bygone. These top-performing televisions are my “desert island” picks, if said island had a power generator and good popcorn.

For more audio and home theater recommendations, check out related guides to the best OLED TVs, the best art TVs, the best RGB TVs, the best home theater projectors, and the best soundbars. If you truly don't know where to start, try our guide on how to buy a TV.

Update November 2026: We've revamped this entire guide, replacing nearly all of our top picks, Honorable Mentions, and Other TVs We've Tested.

The great debate: which size television is best for your living room? Does it even matter? The first thing to consider is price: an 80-inch TV is going to cost significantly more than a 65-inch one. In some cases, that means an extra grand or two for the privilege of seeing the stubble on Spider-Man’s face in great detail. Broadly speaking, the larger the room, the more useful a large screen is. The most popular size is 65 inches, which is a great option for most medium sizes spaces.

I’m partial to art televisions for one simple reason: They are useful even when they are turned off. Art TVs use an antireflective layer on top of the screen to create more realistic, textured still images like artwork or paintings. The best ones can replace a picture frame or wall art in any room, and double as great TVs.

TV tech has evolved in 2026, and the new kid on the block is RGB TVs. These new models shine red, blue, and green light through an LCD panel to create brighter, more vibrant color. OLED on the other hand can control the color of individual pixels and even turn them off completely, leading to excellent contrast and black levels.

Which one performs the best? That depends. RGB models like the LG Micro RGB Evo provide more control over picture quality, brightness, and contrast. OLED televisions like the Samsung S95H tend to look better on movie night, especially for nighttime scenes when inky blacks need to look realistic. If pressed, I’d pick an RGB TV model because you get more control over color, but that's just me.

I picked Samsung's The Frame Pro (2026) as the best overall TV because it has stunningly good picture quality and looks incredible showcasing a masterpiece painting on your wall. I remember staring at the 65-inch model on the wall of my office for two months, just marveling at high-res shipwreck photos and the real wood bezels that encompass the screen (and snap easily into place, by the way). With just about every painting, The Frame Pro showed intricate and detailed textures, as though you could reach out and feel the waves of an ocean or curve of a face.

The Frame Pro is also an excellent choice for movies and shows. Because of the anti-glare screen and matte finish, the dark corridors of scenes in The Batman on HBO had extra depth and animated movies like Toy Story 5 on Disney+ popped with color. Sure, it doesn't have the true black pixels of an OLED screen, but its other capabilities more than make up for it.

Extra features included easy-to-install stands (no screws needed) and a breakout box called the Samsung Wireless One Connect that lets you connect streaming devices and a game console from across the room. That means fewer wires dangling on the wall.

TCL is a more budget-focused television maker that tends to undercut the price of brands like Samsung and LG. A good example of this is the TCL QM7L Mini-LED TV above, which has a budget-friendly price but anything but oriented picture quality. If anything, this QLED model competes with some OLED TVs for inky, deep blacks and rich color. Will you notice any contrast issues when you watch the latest Pixar film? Probably not, especially since the QM7L also looked exceptionally bright in our tests.

A few other extras to mention: We loved the backlit remote, which makes it easier to punch up the volume or adjust picture quality settings in a dark room. You might even be able to skip a soundbar with this TV—the internal Bang & Olufsen speakers sound nice and loud.

Samsung, TCL, Hisense, and Sony released new RGB TVs this year, but it's the LG Micro RGB Evo that takes the crown, namely because you can tweak so many of its picture settings. In my tests,I could tweak the picture so much that movies and shows that mostly take place at night still looked visible, clear, and realistic. As one example among many, I remember tweaking settings for the movie Awake, a largely dark film, to the point where it looked clear and colorful. Other RGB models from Hisense and even Samsung struggled with those same dark scenes, no matter which setting I used.

Another major plus is that this model supports 100 percent of the color gamut specs for BT.2020, DCI-P3, and Adobe RGB. In layman's terms, if you watch a movie like Guardians of the Galaxy Vol. 2 (which also matches those specs), you will see a true rainbow of every color available, with unmatched color vibrancy. This TV is so striking that I came away thinking that OLED TVs are no longer the obvious gold standard in 2026.

Samsung made a big change to their flagship OLED model this year, the Samsung S95H. They ditch the Samsung One Connect breakout box that was required with previous models (and is still required on TVs like The Frame Pro). The TV now has ports directly on the panel, where you can connect video game consoles, streaming devices, or Blu-ray players directly. While that might create more cable clutter, I found it much easier to use than the previous Samsung S95F.

Perhaps more importantly, the S95H is a top performer when it comes to picture quality and settings. I could adjust the contrast and brightness to perfectly light famously dim shows like Game of Thrones. As an OLED, the blacks look richer and darker, more so than most RGB models. The AI optimization picture settings here are actually useful—during soccer matches, the TV adjust to make the pitch look even greener and minimizes background crowd noise, for example.

It's a bit pricey, but the overall brightness, contrast, color quality, and even built-in sound are all premium-grade.

The company famous for streaming sticks released its first OLED TV this year, and it follows the brand's formula of low-cost simplicity. Roku has, up until this point, stuck with LED TV models with average performance. Wonder of wonders, the Pro Series is not bad at all, making even dark scenes in movies like Solo: A Star Wars Story look vibrant and colorful. Outside of picture quality, Roku makes things as easy as possible, with features like Smart Picture Max, which automatically tweaks picture settings for you, a simple TV operating system (Roku OS, of course), and an uncluttered remote.

I will say, the brightness and contrast are good for this price, but nowhere near the cinema-quality color of something like the premium Samsung S95H. I think it's a great option for anyone on a budget or for people who only watch TV occasionally.

The Samsung The Frame Pro (2026) is my TV of the year, but the Amazon Ember Artline is also a capable, significantly cheaper, art TV. Just don't expert as premium of an image in movies and TV shows.

Art televisions use a matte finish on the screen to help make paintings look more realistic and textured. The Ember Artline achieves this look, and even includes frame bezel that snap into place around the screen (these are sold separately on Samsung's Frame TVs). I loved looking at painting on this TV, including some animated ones Amazon offers in the catalog. Using the TV is simple, and the improved Alexa+ voice assistant is useful for changing the volume (hands free) or finding obscure sci-fi films.

Image quality isn't the big selling point of this TV: the art, aesthetic, and low price are. Gamers will find the 60 Hz refresh rate lacking, especially for twitchy action titles. Contrast and brightness are okay, but not nearly as impressive as something like the Samsung The Frame Pro. But keep in mind: this TV is far, far cheaper than premium options.

While the LG Micro RGB Evo is endlessly tweakable and outperformed every other RGB model I tested for picture quality, the Sony BRAVIA 9 II RGB TV still takes the cake when it comes to gaming. That’s especially true if you own a Sony PlayStation 5, since the TV will automatically adjust the picture quality and latency to match the game console. I tested it mostly using a high-end PC, and at a 120 Hz refresh rate, latency felt non-existent to me.

I also found this TV to be a capable alternative to a premium OLED. Red, green, and blue colors are almost infinitely adjustable. During a scene in Toy Story 5 by a tree in a field, the sun creates splashes of color that look incredibly lush and vivid. Watching Game of Thrones, I found I could enable a special black level setting to make night battles look more realistic.

The Bravia 9 II uses Google TV OS and Google Gemini voice assistant, which both run simply, smoothly, and with no weird hiccups. While the LG Micro RGB Evo is a better performer for color and contrast, the BRAVIA 9 II’s gaming features, fast performance, high-end speakers, and deep blacks are exceptional.

Hisense UR9 RGB Mini-LED: My top pick for a midrange RGB, the Hisense UR9 has rich colors thanks to RGB tech shining red, green, and blue pixels. Some scenes, including ones in the movie The Last Duel, looked extra crisp and colorful. But it's not quite as excellent as my top picks.

LG B6 OLED: The LG B5 is a great budget-friendly OLED TV that performed very similarly to the Roku Pro Series OLED. Either one of them would serve you well, but we gave the nod to the Roku TV for it's lower price, simpler remote, and easy to use Roku operating system.

Samsung Micro RGB R95H: Samsung's Micro RGB display tech didn’t make the Samsung Micro RGB R95H stand out from the competition as much as I'd hoped. In fact, the LG Micro RGB Evo responded much better to picture quality adjustments, and while shows and movies looked colorful and clear, they weren't quite as jaw-dropping as similarly priced RGB and OLED competitors.

Sony Bravia 7 II: The Sony Bravia 7 II is unfortunately outclassed by the other midrange RGB TV on this list: the Hisense UR9. It is more expensive, and it's harder to set up, and the picture quality doesn't quite match that of the Hisense TV.

Sony Bravia 8 II: Former WIRED reviewer Ryan Waniata tested this stunning high-end OLED, and praised it's top-notch image quality and processing. That said, the Samsung S95H is newer, about the same price, and has a more striking, glare-free image than the Sony Bravia 8 II.

TCL Nxtvision Series Art: This budget art TV simply cannot compete with similarly low-priced art TVs like the Amazon Ember Artline TV and Hisense Class S7 CanvasTV. Both offer better picture quality and a better general user experience. You can read my full review here.

TCL RM9L RGB Mini-LED: The TCL RM9L RGB TV is huge and hugely expensive. The $6,000 85-inch is the smallest and cheapest model you can get, and it is generally outclassed by the LG Micro RGB Evo.

The WIRED reviews team is busy testing the ever-popular midrange LG C6 and premium LG G6. Given past performances from LG C and G-series TVs, we expect them to be strong contenders for top accolades in this guide.
