Airplane Turbulence Is Extremely Hard to Predict, but Maybe Not for Long

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In the early 1500s, an Italian polymath named Leonardo da Vinci took a good crack at exactly the phenomenon that makes nervous fliers clutch their airplane seats in fear and flight attendants push their carts back to the galleys with haste: turbulence.

Da Vinci was fascinated by swirling, chaotic eddies of water, movements so complex that he made hundreds of drawings of his observations. Why, exactly, did water—any fluid, really, be it atmospheric air or blood in the heart—move in that way?

Now the world has ChatGPT and self-driving cars and solar farms, and it still doesn’t exactly know how turbulence works. “It’s the hardest remaining problem in classical physics, and it’s one we need to fly through,” says Michael Murillo, a professor of theoretical and computational physics at Michigan State University.

Now a bumper crop of data-driven efforts to predict and avoid turbulence could get fliers closer to a smooth flight. Twenty terabytes of data are created by an airplane’s engine in one hour of commercial flying, according to the International Air Transport Association, with more broadcast by airplanes midflight: GPS position, altitude, speed. An “electronic flight bag,” basically a specialized iPad, is equipped with navigation charts and manuals and can transmit its own information, as long as the airplane’s internet connection cooperates. Satellites watch and collect it all.

“Air traffic has gone up, and communication costs and the costs of putting computers on aircraft have come down,” says Larry Cornman, a physicist with the National Science Foundation’s National Center for Atmospheric Research who has been studying turbulence for decades. “The time is now to work on these things because we can do them.”

This is good news for aviation safety. More than 200 people were seriously injured on US airplanes in turbulence-related incidents between 2009 and 2023, according to FAA data. Turbulence is also getting worse. Climate change alters the atmospheric conditions around the world’s jet streams, creating destabilizing wind shear. From the cockpit of an airplane, this translates into sudden, clear-air turbulence, difficult to see or predict. Historically, pilots’ only method of avoiding turbulence is heeding the warning of those who accidentally went through it before them, and hoping that nothing had changed.

But just in the past two years, Cornman’s decades of work on turbulence has been put into the field in ways it couldn’t be before, part of several projects coordinated by the US federal government and the airline industry. One breakthrough came when Cornman and an FAA air traffic controller determined that aircrafts’ federally required broadcasts of their positions, altitude, and speed could be used to spot unexpected turbulence, without requiring new hardware or software onboard. Researchers can use this crowdsourced, public data to make predictions about when and where turbulence will pop up.

In September, the National Science Foundation announced a $30 million award to nine universities to stand up a new center dedicated to predicting turbulence—in the air, but also in the charged particles inside a fusion reactor, and in the gas clouds that stretch across space.

“Turbulence is the reason we don’t have fusion energy yet,” says Murillo, who heads up the new center, called Transformative Explorations in Multi-Physics and Engineering of Scientific Turbulence, or Tempest. The chaotic swirling of superhot plasma inside a reactor allows heat to leak out, preventing the material from reaching the temperatures needed to allow nuclei to fuse. The US federal government wants to get closer to figuring that out.

“Closer,” because turbulence is impossible to perfectly predict, scientists say. For one thing, turbulence occurs at many different, small scales at the same time. Air swirling around an airplane breaks into eddies, then smaller eddies, and then even smaller eddies, with each interacting with each other—too many variables, happening too quickly, to mathematically account for. For another, tiny changes—wind speed, temperature—can eventually dramatically alter air movement, suddenly altering the turbulence once predicted. To make perfect forecasts, one would need a perfect picture of exactly what’s going on in the atmosphere at all times.

But increasing sophisticated applications of machine learning could allow scientists to get closer to knowing what’s going to happen when, says Murillo. And while brute, computational force can’t solve the turbulence problem, AI agents might give the effort a boost. OpenAI’s blockbuster announcement this fall that it had resolved the Navier-Stokes problem—equations that are the beginning of all turbulence models—suggests LLMs could help.

“Turbulence has been a problem only human minds could work on; as of this year, that's no longer true,” says Murillo.

To make flying better, the aviation industry doesn’t necessarily need to solve turbulence. It needs to get better at knowing what sort of conditions create it and not send airplanes there.

One company in Tel Aviv, SkyPath, uses those in-plane iPads and their accelerometers to collect and share crowdsourced data between different airplanes, even if they don’t fly for the same airline. (This sort of dating sharing can be a sensitive topic for competing companies; SkyPath says it assuages these concerns by ensuring that data is anonymized before it’s shared.) More than 40,000 of these devices have SkyPath installed on them, says CEO Maya Shpak; the company says that several major airlines, including American, use the technology. SkyPath combines that collected data with additional information, including weather forecasts, and uses machine intelligence to make predictions about the future state of the air. The app helps pilots avoid turbulent air and warn passengers and crew members that choppiness is coming, 10 to 15 minutes before it happens.

One pilot named Eric who uses SkyPath, who asked WIRED not to use his full name because he was not authorized to speak to the media, said the app is a helpful tool that allows him to warn passengers what to expect. His issue is the same as those studying turbulence: One can’t take measurements everywhere all at once.

“It’s like any crowdsourced software,” says the pilot. “It only works if everyone uses it, and no one wants to use it until everyone else is.” Today, several large operators are using the app, which makes it especially useful in areas with lots of flights, he says.

Cornman, the physicist, wants to keep all these advances in perspective. “No matter what people say, we’re not getting close to 100 percent, 90 percent, 80 percent [predictive accuracy] anytime soon,” he says. “It’s too complicated a problem.” Smoother skies, if not perfect ones, are ahead.

Your AI Strategy Has A Single Point Of Failure: Convenience Is How AI Concentration Begins

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

KPMG Partner | Global Data & AI Leader | Enterprise AI, indirect tax compliance, and finance transformation across global organizations.

Ask most executives where their AI risk sits, and they point to the model: bias, hallucination, privacy. Those risks are real, but another exposure can disable several well-governed systems at once: concentration.

A few months ago, I sat with the finance leadership team of a global company running more than a dozen AI use cases. I asked which model sat underneath each one. It took them two weeks to answer. Nearly all traced back to one provider, through three vendor products nobody had connected.

No one designed that dependency; it built up one sensible decision at a time. Concentration arrives as convenience.

Supply chain leaders know that two suppliers offer little protection if both rely on the same factory. I apply the same scrutiny to AI.

I trace each critical workflow through its application vendor, model and version, hosting platform, region, identity service, retrieval store and data sources. I record the owners, the contractual counterparty and the processing locations. I ask vendors to disclose their upstream dependencies and say plainly what they can’t see.

Then I ask which workflows could fail together. Different vendors may share a model provider. Different models may share a cloud region. A backup route that relies on the failed identity service is no backup.

Concentration becomes a single point of failure when one shared dependency can stop a critical process with no acceptable workaround. Counting vendors tells me very little.

I prioritize by business impact, not model popularity. A meeting summarizer and a regulatory filing workflow deserve different protection.

For every critical process, I want the business to agree on maximum tolerable downtime, recovery time, acceptable data loss and minimum service quality during disruption, along with thresholds for accuracy, latency, throughput and cost per transaction. An invoice workflow, for example, might require recovery within four hours, no lost approved transactions and human review whenever required fields fail validation. Those figures are illustrative. The process owner must approve the real limits.

I also make sure someone has the authority to suspend automation, authorize fallback and speak to affected teams. Recovery targets without decision rights are aspirations.

Outages are visible, but behavioral changes are harder to see. On one engagement, a document processing workflow began placing fields incorrectly after an upstream model update. Nothing crashed, and it took weeks to find the cause. When several workflows share that dependency, one update can spread errors across the business.

I pin model versions where supported, track retirement dates and record prompts, retrieval settings, tool definitions and model identifiers with each release. Pinning buys control, not permanent availability.

I also insist on a representative evaluation set with expected answers, difficult cases and business acceptance thresholds. We run it before releases and periodically against production to catch unannounced changes. We measure field accuracy and business-rule compliance, not just whether a response parses. Failures go to human review, and we decide in advance when to roll back, switch models or pause processing.

I monitor service errors separately from output quality because a healthy endpoint can still deliver unusable results. Where recovery targets can’t be met economically, I ask the business to accept the exposure explicitly, with its consequences and a review date. The chief data and AI officer (CDAIO) should make that trade-off visible rather than inherit it.

I keep prompts, evaluation sets, source documents, output schemas and business logic under our control, and I isolate provider-specific calls behind adapters. However, a common interface doesn’t make models interchangeable. An alternative may need different prompts, tool permissions or retrieval settings. Changing embedding models may require rebuilding the index, and fine-tuning assets or managed agent state may not transfer.

I test the whole workflow, including access controls, audit records, data residency, downstream integrations and capacity under expected demand. A demonstration with 10 documents doesn’t prove recovery capability.

The fallback must match the failure. Another region addresses a regional outage, and another provider addresses provider loss. Neither fixes a shared authentication failure. For some processes, a practiced manual queue is the best option, with defined staffing, backlog limits and reconciliation so transactions are neither lost nor processed twice.

I ask procurement, legal and engineering to review change and retirement notices, dependency disclosures, export formats, termination assistance and our rights to retain prompts, evaluation assets and tuning data. Then I test the export. Portability on paper without usable files and a working destination is weak protection. I also weigh resilience spending against the loss it reduces because blanket duplication can consume budget without removing the dependency that matters.

In the first 30 days, I work with business owners to inventory critical workflows, map shared dependencies and approve recovery tolerances. Undisclosed dependencies go on the log as risks, each with an owner and a deadline.

By the 60th day, engineering and operations set evaluation baselines, verify capacity, identify contractual barriers and write fallback runbooks with activation triggers, decision authority and reconciliation checks.

By the 90th day, we simulate provider unavailability and a failed quality threshold. We measure detection time, switching time, recovered throughput, output quality and backlog clearance. Then we assign remediation and repeat the exercise according to criticality and after material changes.

I give directors one page: critical processes exposed to shared dependencies, recovery targets against demonstrated results, unresolved exceptions and accountable owners.

​The revealing question is whether we’ve shown that essential work can continue when the first vendor fails or changes. The next stage of CDAIO leadership will be defined by what we can withstand. Resilience is credible only when it’s measured, rehearsed and owned.​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

Donald Trump Says It’s Not Too Late for The US to Buy Nvidia Shares

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Log in to access your notifications and stay updated. If you’re not a member yet, Sign Up to get started!

Rizwan is an experienced Crypto journalist with almost half a decade of experience covering everything related to the growing crypto industry — from price analysis to blockchain disruption. During this period, he’s authored more than 3,000 news articles for Coinpedia News.

Rizwan is an experienced Crypto journalist with almost half a decade of experience covering everything related to the growing crypto industry — from price analysis to blockchain disruption. During this period, he’s authored more than 3,000 news articles for Coinpedia News.

Trump said the U.S. government could still consider buying a stake in Nvidia.

Jensen Huang is scheduled to face congressional hearings on December 1 and 3.

Despite this news, Nvidia shares fell 5.86% to $155.38 after a sharp previous-day rally.

President Donald Trump has opened the door to a possible U.S. government stake in Nvidia, saying “it’s probably not too late” when asked about buying equity in the AI chipmaker.

The comments add a new twist to Washington’s growing role in America’s critical semiconductor industry. Its shares fell 5.86% to $155.38 on November 24.

In an exclusive interview, Trump said he had considered having the U.S. government buy a stake in Nvidia, similar to the nearly 10% stake it took in Intel in 2025.

He made the comments while reviewing Nvidia’s live stock prices at the Resolute Desk in the White House.

$NVDA – TRUMP OPEN TO PARDONING JENSEN HUANG, CONSIDERS U.S. STAKE IN NVIDIAPresident Donald Trump told the New York Post he would consider granting Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang a presidential pardon if necessary, although he doubts one will be needed.“It’s always an option, I…

Trump said he had considered taking an equity position in Nvidia but had not yet raised a formal proposal.

“I thought about it. I said, ‘You know, maybe I should do it,’ but I didn’t bring it up at this point. It’s probably not too late.”

This idea would give Washington a direct financial interest in Nvidia while increasing the government’s role in one of the companies at the center of the global AI chip race.

However, Trump’s comments do not mean a deal is ongoing.

The potential equity stake comes as Nvidia faces growing scrutiny over CEO Jensen Huang’s alleged links to a suspected Chinese spy and national security concerns over its advanced AI chips.

The U.S. already receives a 15% to 25% share of revenue from certain approved Nvidia AI chip sales to China.

Huang is also preparing to face congressional hearings on December 1 and 3, where lawmakers are expected to question him about chip exports and national security. Trump urged him to tell the truth rather than use his Fifth Amendment right against self-incrimination, saying,

“I don’t think he needs to take the Fifth… I think I want him to just tell the truth.”

Trump was also asked whether he would consider pardoning Huang if he faced federal charges linked to the alleged scandal.

“It’s always an option, I guess, but I don’t think it’ll be necessary.”

Despite Trump considering buying Nvidia shares, the stock failed to rise. NVDA closed at $155.38 on November 24, down 5.86%, after jumping 31.5% the previous day.

The stock had climbed from a 52-week low of $124.50 on November 20 to $165.05 on November 23 before falling again.

If the U.S. government buys a stake in Nvidia, it would be a major change for the company.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Mario Kart World just got a massive new update in version 1.9.0, finally bringing Nintendo's Switch 2 racing game into the limelight – but not all fans are satisfied.

Nintendo unveils 1.9.0 in a colorful trailer on YouTube, featuring eight "new" tracks and two Knockout Tour routes – and yes, the quote marks around the word "new" are intentional on my part. That's because the tracks are sequels of sorts, acting as the "two" counterparts to already existing maps: Airship Fortress 2, Desert Hills 2, DK Pass 2, Maio Bros. Circuit 2, Koopa Troopa Beach 2, Salty Salty Speedway 2, and Wario Stadium 2.

Fans are jokingly calling 1.9.0 the "update of 2s" in the comments, noting that there isn't one truly new map in the lineup here. They're just reverse takes on old tracks, really. "This update certainly has 'The Power of Two' in it," reads one reply. Another jests, "This is like if McDonald's slapped a McChicken inside a McDouble, put it in a new box, and called it a brand-new menu item," clarifying they're "not mad at this update, though."

There is still 2.0 to look forward to, after all – and players note that's likely why Nintendo hasn't dropped anything too big in 1.9.0. "People don't seem to realize we're getting close to a 2.0 update," as one response puts it. "That's the big one that will have a lot of stuff that probably people are looking for and stuff that people aren't expecting." As for the Knockout Tour routes, this week's patch also brings two: Feather Rally and Lucky Cat Rally.

The new version includes plenty of other changes, as outlined on Nintendo's official support website. They range from technical to wholesome, like the fact that "confetti now falls during the results presentation in Grand Prix and Knockout Tour." Folks can also now pass through the river route to Cheep Cheep Falls from DK Pass, Sky-High Sundae, Salty Salty Speedway, Starview Peak, and Wario Shipyard.

All in all, it's not a bad update – heck, at least it's something seeing the love Nintendo's given Mario Kart 8 recently – but it does leave ample room for a banger 2.0, if you ask me.

Hyperliquid Leads Altcoin Losses as Bitcoin Slips Below $59K

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Hyperliquid Leads Altcoin Losses as Bitcoin Slips Below $59K

Hyperliquid's HYPE token led crypto's latest leg down on Wednesday, as one of the exchange's most profitable traders shut down its operations there.

HYPE fell 8.2% over 24 hours to $57.86, the steepest drop among the 20 largest cryptocurrencies by market cap, and is down 15.8% over the past week, according to Decrypt data. Bitcoin slipped 1.7% over the same period to $58,801, and Ethereum lost 2.4%, extending a sell-off that has spread across risk assets.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short HYPE with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

Abraxas Capital has withdrawn its remaining funds from Hyperliquid and closed its entire book, almost two years after it started trading there, pseudonymous onchain analyst MLM said on Telegram on Tuesday.

The firm traded $13.4 billion on Hyperliquid and made $530 million, including at least $67 million from funding payments, according to MLM. The positions were one leg of a market-neutral strategy for its Elysium Global Arbitrage Fund, the analyst said.

Two Hyperliquid accounts labeled as Abraxas Capital on analytics platform Hyperdash have recorded combined all-time profits of more than $550 million, and now hold just over $5,000 between them, Hyperdash data shows. On Tuesday, the two accounts unstaked about 219,000 HYPE, worth roughly $12.7 million at current prices, and sent it elsewhere.

"With HYPE, upcoming token unlocks could be adding extra pressure," Eric Andersson, co-founder of Byrrgis, told Decrypt. "Altcoins tend to get hit even harder in these situations."

U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs recorded $231.6 million in net outflows on Tuesday, their sixth consecutive day of withdrawals, according to SoSoValue data. Outflows over those six trading days total $2.6 billion.

That takes November's net outflows to $4.94 billion, surpassing June 2026's $4.51 billion as the funds' worst month since they launched in January 2024. Total net assets stand at $72 billion.

Andersson pointed to ETF outflows, uncertainty over interest rates, liquidations and weaker sentiment across financial markets as drivers of Bitcoin's decline.

Options traders have turned increasingly bearish. Over the past week, 1.7 put options, which pay out when prices fall, changed hands for every call on a seven-day average, the most since June 2022, Glassnode said in its weekly report. Measured by open interest, the ratio is at its highest since May 2021.

Bitcoin is back at its summer lows near $60K, and stocks are also in a downtrend.

BTC gave back the whole NVIDIA rally in less than six hours, an indication of severe relative weakness.

More on capitulation and leverage in this week's Week On-chain. https://t.co/GwEUc1pqHW

— glassnode (@glassnode) November 24, 2026

Such readings do not always mark a bottom. In June 2022, a similar reading coincided with a price low, but the bear market's final low was still five months away, while the open interest peak in April 2021 came near a market top, Glassnode noted.

Glassnode also said options dealers are "short gamma" between $58,000 and $64,000, meaning their hedging requires them to sell as prices fall and buy as they rise, which can make moves in either direction larger. Just outside that range, their hedging would slow a move, the firm said.

Glassnode's report found that Bitcoin investors are locking in their heaviest losses since the collapse of FTX.

A clearer outlook on interest rates and renewed ETF inflows could help bring stability back to the market, Andersson said.

Huawei convicted on 10 counts in U.S. trial, but verdict is unlikely to slow China’s ‘national champion’

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A federal jury in New York convicted Huawei on 10 of 12 counts Tuesday, including bank fraud, wire fraud, and conspiracy to steal trade secrets. Yet Huawei was acquitted of racketeering conspiracy and of conspiracy to obstruct justice.

The verdict caps a U.S. case launched in 2018 that accused the Chinese telecommunications giant of misleading banks about its business in Iran while stealing trade secrets from U.S. companies. Sentencing is scheduled for September 2027.

“Few crimes are as serious as Huawei’s decades-long pattern of endangering U.S. national security, violating the sanctity of U.S. commerce, and impeding the enforcement of U.S. laws,” U.S. Attorney Joseph Nocella Jr said in a statement.

“Today’s verdict is a step towards accountability for Huawei’s criminal activities, which includes yearslong collaboration with sanctioned entities in Iran, repeated lies to banks, and blatant thefts from U.S. technology companies,” he added.

Brian Heberlig, a lawyer for Huawei, told reporters that the acquittal on the racketeering charge reflected the company’s “good-faith compliance culture.”

“We fully expect to appeal,” Heberlig told reporters, according to Reuters.

In a statement, Huawei argued that the acquittal on the racketeering charge “confirms that these allegations lack factual basis.” The company also said it believed the other charges “are equally without legal or factual merit,” and that it would “take all necessary legal actions to defend our rights and interests.”

The Chinese embassy in Washington did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

Meng Wanzhou, Huawei’s chief financial officer and the daughter of founder Ren Zhengfei, was arrested in Canada in December 2018 at the request of U.S. authorities. Prosecutors accused her of misleading banks about Huawei’s relationship with Skycom, a subsidiary operating in Iran. After nearly three years fighting extradition, she was released in September 2021 under a deferred prosecution agreement with U.S. prosecutors; the charges were later dismissed.

Separately, the U.S. Commerce Department placed Huawei on its Entity List in 2019, restricting its access to American goods, software and technology. Washington tightened those controls in 2020 to cover certain foreign-made products manufactured using U.S. technology or software.

Tuesday’s verdict is unlikely to slow Huawei, Dexter Tiff Roberts, a nonresident senior fellow at the Atlantic Council’s Global China Hub, says. “Huawei has already weathered years of sanctions and still been able to grow and develop remarkably, even with limited access to a number of developed country markets and in particular the U.S.”

Beijing may double down on Huawei instead, he adds. “In some ways, the adversity it’s already faced with sanctions [has] made Beijing double down on supporting it as a national champion, and if anything, the latest court ruling will only strengthen Beijing’s resolve to support Huawei to ensure it does not stumble.”

The verdict comes as Huawei takes on a growing role in China’s push to build a domestic AI-computing industry, amid U.S. export controls blocking the country’s access to Nvidia’s most advanced chips. The company’s Ascend semiconductors are China’s best hope for the AI processors needed to train and run models. Chinese models are increasingly being designed to run on Huawei chips, even if Nvidia’s are better for training.

Huawei rotating chair Eric Xu said the company cannot produce enough AI chips and servers to meet demand in mainland China. “Since we don’t have enough capacity to even satisfy the demand in China, we don’t have a plan to expand into the international market in a fully-fledged way,” Xu told reporters at Huawei Connect in Shanghai in September, according to Reuters.

Huawei reported 880.9 billion yuan ($126 billion) in revenue last year, according to its annual report.

November's full moon dazzles skywatchers with a stunning Beaver Moon supermoon (photos)

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

From the skyscrapers of New York to the ancient Parthenon in Athens, the Beaver Moon supermoon put on a spectacular show for skywatchers around the world.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The November full moon rose to put on a spectacular natural light show on Nov. 24 and we've got the photos to prove it! Read on to see our favorite shots of the "Beaver Moon" captured from around the world.

Each monthly full moon has its own set of informal names, often bestowed to reflect events occurring in the world at the time of their rising. The November moon is often known as the Beaver Moon, a name associated with the time of year when beavers prepare for winter and when trappers historically set beaver traps before waterways froze.

November's full moon served as the opening salvo in a series of four dazzling supermoons, which turn full around the time that the natural satellite passes closest to Earth in its 27-day elliptical orbit.

That added proximity can make it appear noticeably brighter and larger than the smallest full moon of the year, setting the stage for an incredible photo shoot as the lunar disk climbed high into the autumn sky.

Our first shot of the moon was captured by photographer Pongmanat Tasiri as the moon rose over the roof of the Wat Phra That Doi Suthep Buddhist temple in Chiang Mai Province, Thailand, as a stunning array of craters and lunar seas scar the Beaver Moon's surface.

Meanwhile, Gary Hershorn snapped a gorgeous shot of the full yellow moon rising over the Chrysler Building in New York, with the Empire State Building and myriad other skyscrapers dominating the skyline.

Hershorn also managed to line up a colorful shot of the lunar disk glowing between the concrete canyon of New York's iconic 42nd Street just before moonset on the morning of Nov. 24, mere hours before the moon turned full.

Our next photo was captured by Space.com's Skywatching Editor Daisy Dobrijevic using a Sony A7IV with a Tamron 50-400mm lens as the moon shone in the skies over the city of Nottingham in the U.K. on Nov. 23.

"Having seen the rather disappointing weather forecast for the night of the full moon, I decided to head out a day early and capture the almost-full Beaver Moon instead," explained Dobrijevic. "It hadn’t quite risen above the rooftops of the houses opposite me, so I took a peek down the alleyway and, sure enough, there it was, nestled between two houses, giving me just enough of a clear view to capture this close-up."

This striking image of the full moon rising above the Toronto skyline was snapped by Mert Alper Dervis as a flock of birds skimmed through the foreground. The outline of the lunar disk appears subtly warped and squashed by atmospheric refraction, as its reflected light passes through pockets of air with differing densities and temperatures.

Allen Berman sent in a breathtaking photo of the full moon hanging low over the Los Angeles skyline on the night of Nov. 24.

"Finding the right spot for tonight’s full moon over downtown Los Angeles was a bit tricky," Berman told Space.com. "We wound up on a neighbor’s front lawn in the Baldwin Hills section of town. The owner was a bit surprised to see photographers there. But he was cool. The conditions were perfect with thin clouds over the distant mountains that made for a magical image."

Moving from the modern to the old, photographer Robert Nemeti took a moody shot of the full Beaver Moon climbing skyward above the medieval Boldogkő Castle in Hungary at moonrise on Nov. 24. How many full moons must that old building have seen?

Wang Zhao snapped a very different view from Beijing, China, on that same night by using a powerful telephoto lens to capture a passenger jet as it streaked across the lunar disk, leaving dense contrails in its wake.

That Beaver Moon was captured shining between the minarets of the Imam Ali Shrine in the city of Najaf, Iraq, by Karar Essa. The 53-mile-wide (85-kilometer) Tycho Crater can be seen as a distinctive bright feature below the lunar equator, close to the dark expanse of Mare Nubium.

Aris Oikonomou snapped a striking photo of the Beaver Moon shining above the Parthenon in Athens, Greece, just as night began to set in over the Acropolis.

Feeling inspired to capture your own supermoon photos? Then be sure to read our guide to photographing the moon and browse our picks of the best cameras and lenses for astrophotography because you don't have long to wait!

The final supermoon of 2026 rises in just a few short weeks on Dec. 23 and it just so happens to be the closest full moon of the year, passing just 221,668 miles (356,740 kilometers) from Earth at the point of maximum illumination.

So no excuses! We'd love to see your photo in our inbox next month.

Editor's Note: If you would like to share your full moon photos with Space.com's readers, then please send your photo(s), comments, name and location to spacephotos@space.com.

Anthony Wood joined Space.com in April 2025 after contributing articles to outlets including IGN, New Atlas and Gizmodo. He has a passion for the night sky, science, Hideo Kojima, and human space exploration, and can’t wait for the day when astronauts once again set foot on the moon.

One of the most sought-after products in Black Friday sales are Apple's iPads. These powerful tablets often cost a lot, and rarely see discounts, so a reduction in price becomes all the more tempting. But if you're just looking at the recent models, you're missing a trick.

Walmart is a major seller of restored iPads, or second-hand slates that have been professionally returned to a near-new condition. Tablets like this are often much cheaper than new ones, and work just as well (you can take my word for it, I bought a refurbished 2020 iPad Pro and it still works perfectly). While these models are cheaper anyway, Walmart is offering Black Friday deals on a fair few of them, bringing tablets down to remarkably cheap prices. These are mostly for older models, with a 2021 release the newest here, but they're still powerful and modern slates and so you might find an older iPad suits you fine.

Perhaps the best refurbished iPad deal is this: over a third off the price of a large-screen iPad. It's the 2020 model, but I can promise it still works fantastically since that's the one I own (and bought refurbed). I wish I could've found it at this price.

If you want a smaller iPad, this is the best deal for you; it's the only serious discount on an iPad mini, and 40% off is great. It's only the 64GB model, but one with cellular connection, so you can easily use cloud storage instead.

This is a much older iPad Pro, so you're buying this discount for the large screen rather than modern specs. It has more storage than many other of these refurbished models, at 128GB.

Another modern-build tablet in Walmart's refurb sales is this 2020 iPad Air, and its discount brings it to the lowest price of any home button-less model on this list. It's for the smaller 64GB model.

This is an older iPad, but it still works well (even without being restored), and so $140 is a really good price for it. The extra bargain is in the accessories you're getting with it: a protective case and charging cable are both included with the tablet.

If you've been looking for a new slate, changing tack in the Black Friday iPad deals to buy an older, refurbished model might seem might seem unthinkable.

Older slates work just well for tasks like word processing, streaming video, communications and simple workflow tasks. Slack has never challenged my 6-year-old iPad Pro. The trappings of newer slates, like advanced chipsets, will only really pay off for intensive tasks like video editing.

What about gaming? From my experience, older tablets still run the most advanced games pretty well. After all, game makers don't want to limit their potential audience by excluding owners of older tech, so they optimize their games to work well on various devices.

The only problem with older tablets is that they are less battery-efficient, even in refurbished models which usually enjoy new batteries. Basically any game more complex than NYT Games will sap battery quite quickly, so I only game on my iPad when I'm at home and near a power supply.

So if you're weighing up between an old or new slate, where you plan to play is the big thing worth considering.

Dell’s revenue doubles to $54.9bn as its AI server backlog hits $132.3bn

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Dell booked $60.2bn of new AI server orders and approved another $25bn for buybacks. Its PC business grew just 3% as customers put servers first.

Dell Technologies raised its revenue forecast for the year to $202bn on Tuesday, up from the $192bn it expected before. The new forecast covers the fiscal year that ends in January. Dell published the figures in its third-quarter results.

Revenue rose 103% to a record $54.9bn in the three months to 30 October. Analysts had expected $49.32bn on average, according to LSEG data cited by Reuters. It was Dell’s third forecast increase this year, Jaspreet Singh and Harshita Mary Varghese reported for Reuters. The shares rose about 5% in extended trading.

Net income rose 266% to $5.7bn, or $8.82 a diluted share. Adjusted earnings were $9.45 a share, against the $6.59 analysts expected. Operating expenses fell to 7% of revenue, which Dell said was the lowest in its 42-year history. The board approved another $25bn for share buybacks, after Dell returned a record $4.9bn to shareholders in the quarter.

“We are now a different company, with Infrastructure Solutions Group (ISG) driving the vast majority of our profit and growth,” Jeff Clarke, Dell’s vice chairman and chief operating officer, said in a statement.

📈 Q3FY27 earnings: Record revenue, record EPS and record Q3 cash flow from ops reflect the strength of our operating model, competitive advantages, speed to market and ability to drive scale. These strengths continue to deliver compounding benefits across the business as demand remains strong.

Catch the #earnings call replay & see the Q3 report📝: https://t.co/gcAYS0rUPh

— Dell Technologies (@Dell) November 24, 2026

The infrastructure unit, which sells servers, storage and networking, grew revenue 198% to $42.0bn. AI servers brought in $22.0bn of that, up 291%. Traditional servers and networking rose 235% to $15.0bn, and storage rose 24% to $4.9bn. The unit’s operating income grew 308% to $7.1bn, a margin of 17.0%, up from 12.4% a year earlier.

Dell booked $60.2bn of AI server orders in the quarter and ended it with a record backlog of $132.3bn. It has booked $180bn of orders over the past four quarters. Most of its new orders are for systems built on Nvidia’s Vera Rubin NVL72, Clarke said in his prepared remarks. Dell now counts more than 7,500 customers for its AI Factory systems, across neoclouds, sovereign projects and enterprises.

“Every AI order is vetted for access to capital, end-customer quality, trade and export compliance, and access to land and power,” Clarke said on the earnings call.

Chief financial officer David Kennedy said Dell checks each customer twice: when it agrees the purchase order, and again before production starts. Dell now expects $78bn of AI server revenue this year, up from its earlier forecast of $74bn. About $23.5bn of that should come in the current quarter.

AI servers carry margins in the mid-single digits, Dell said, according to Bloomberg’s Dina Bass. As those sales grow, the infrastructure unit’s operating margin will narrow from 17% to just below 15% in the current quarter, Bloomberg reported.

Kennedy told Bloomberg Television that Dell had seen no slowdown or impact from the brief ouster of Nvidia’s Jensen Huang. Huang was reinstated on Monday. He said the AI backlog was also spreading across more types of customers.

“It’s double-digit number of countries, double-digit number of states across many customers,” Kennedy said.

The PC business grew far more slowly. Revenue in the client unit rose 3% to $12.9bn, after 20% growth in the previous quarter, Reuters reported. Consumer revenue fell 15% to $1.6bn, and the unit’s operating income fell 6% to $704m. Dell said customers were putting their budgets into servers and storage and putting off some PC upgrades. Clarke said on the call that Dell was moving parts it had ordered for PCs into servers, The Register reported.

Dell expects the memory chip shortage to last into 2027, according to Reuters. Dell was also one of the AI server makers that are raising prices after signing deals, the Financial Times reported this week.

“As we look into next year, the gap between component supply and end-user demand continues to widen,” Clarke said.

Dell expects revenue of about $56.25bn in the current quarter, up 69% from a year earlier. In May, its first-quarter results showed an AI server backlog of $51.3bn.

These are the three shows I'd put at the top of my watchlist

There’s nothing better than finding a new show to get stuck into, especially when you’re staring down a long list of streaming options and have absolutely no idea where to start. Thankfully, Prime Video’s latest top 10 chart is doing some of the hard work for us. There are plenty of returning shows holding their ground, alongside newer arrivals that are clearly catching some attention. But as I always say to my fellow friends and family, just because a show is trending doesn’t necessarily mean it’s worth your time.

That’s why I’ve been digging through this week’s most-watched picks to find the ones that genuinely deserve a spot on your watchlist. I’ve narrowed things down to three shows I’d happily recommend to anyone, including an epic fantasy adventure reaching its season finale, a compelling sports drama charting the early life of one of the greatest boxers of all time, and a romantic drama packed with some awkward relationship complications.

This list is based on the Prime Video U.S. top 10 shows as of Wednesday, November 25, 2026.

“The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power” is wrapping up its eight-episode third season, with the final two episodes now available to stream. There’s even better news for fans, as Prime Video renewed the series for season 4 ahead of the finale, so there’s more Middle-earth to look forward to. The show has certainly made headlines for its enormous budget and ambitious production, with Amazon originally committing to a five-season plan and involving the Tolkien Estate in overseeing the adaptation. It’s a fascinating chapter in the franchise’s television history, and thankfully, the story isn’t over yet.

Set five years after the fall of Eregion, the third season finds Middle-earth engulfed in war as Sauron’s (Charlie Vickers) armies continue their march across the land. With the Dark Lord establishing his stronghold in Mordor, he sets his sights on forging the One Ring, a powerful weapon that could secure his control over Middle-earth. Meanwhile, Galadriel (Morfydd Clark), Elrond (Robert Aramayo), Gandalf (Daniel Weyman) and their allies face mounting challenges as they attempt to unite the Elves, Dwarves and Men against him. As battles spread across the kingdoms, Sauron also works to enslave his ring-servants.

Watch "The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power" on Prime Video

“The Greatest” is still holding strong in Prime Video’s top 10, a biographical sports drama following four formative years in the life of Cassius Clay as he goes from promising amateur boxer to heavyweight champion Muhammad Ali. Rather than focusing entirely on his career in the ring, the series also explores the personal, family and cultural pressures that shaped him along the way. This is a more intimate approach to Ali’s story, while the faithful recreation of historic boxing footage also helps bring the story to life. It’s an interesting watch even if you’re not a huge boxing fan.

Watford appoint Iordanescu as head coach

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Edward Iordanescu took Romania to the last 16 at Euro 2024

Watford have appointed former Romania boss Edward Iordanescu as the Championship club's new head coach.

The 48-year-old replaces Alessio Dionisi, who was sacked on Sunday after only five months in charge following a run of seven defeats in their past 10 league games, culminating in Saturday's 4-0 loss to Sheffield United.

"I am very proud and happy to be at this football club," Iordanescu told the club website., external

"I can see the team is in a difficult moment but I have already watched many of the games over the last few days and I'm very confident about the future."

He becomes Watford's sixth new boss in less than 18 months and is the 25th manager since the Pozzo family took over the club in 2012.

Pioneer or coach-eater? The complex world of Pozzo's Watford

The former CFR Cluj and Legia Warsaw manager will be joined at Watford by backroom staff Alexandru Radu, Grzegorz Mokry and Michele Iannucci, but will watch Wednesday's Championship match at home to Blackburn Rovers from the stands before taking charge for Saturday's game at Derby.

Adrian Mariappa will oversee Wednesday's match at Vicarage Road (19:45 GMT), alongside Paul Furlong and Lloyd Doyley.

The Watford job is Iordanescu's first since leaving Legia Warsaw in October 2025, where he won the Super Cup last season.

It is the 10th managerial post of his 15-year coaching career, which has taken in Bulgarian club CSKA Sofia as well as Steaua Bucharest and Cluj - who he guided to the Romanian title and two domestic cups after joining midway through the 2020-21 season.

He also spent two-and-a-half years in charge of Romania, guiding them to the last 16 of Euro 2024, where they were beaten by the Netherlands.

Iordanescu takes over a Watford side currently three points above the relegation zone, having won only four of their 16 games so far.

Watford will be Iordanescu's first job in English football

Although untried in English football, Watford are getting a trophy-winning head coach with a wide-ranging CV spanning spells in his homeland, Bulgaria and Poland, as well as elite-level international experience with Romania.

His connections with his national side run deep as the son of his country's legendary former head coach Anghel Iordanescu, who famously took them to the quarter-finals of the 1994 World Cup during a reign of more than 100 games.

It is likely he will need every bit of that nous and inspiration as he becomes the latest boss to try to revive a club which continues to plough through managers at an alarming rate.

Dionisi was Watford's 12th head coach, not including interim bosses, since the end of the 2020-21 season, with Iordanescu becoming the sixth permanent boss since Tom Cleverley was sacked in May 2025.

The Hornets' slow start to the season - with only four home wins and one point out of 24 away - has played out against the backdrop of uncertainty and unrest off the pitch.

Watford's owner, Gino Pozzo, is currently standing trial in Spain as one of a number of defendants accused of tax offences relating to Spanish team Granada, a club he used to be associated with.

He denies the allegations, which are related to player trading at Granada.

The case continues a tumultuous period for the club, with supporter and former owner Elton John voicing his frustration after a 4-0 home defeat by Burnley last month.

Fans have also questioned the running of the club, with one supporters' group declaring a loss of confidence in the ownership.

Iordanescu's immediate concern will be improving results, particularly Watford's form on the road, where they have yet to win and have conceded 18 goals in eight matches - with only Burnley letting in more.

He arrives with a reputation for defensive organisation and stability, which the Hornets are in deseprate need of.

Geoff Doyle, BBC Three Counties Radio sports editor

So Watford try again with a new head coach - it is a regular occurrence at Vicarage Road.

Iordanescu has a decent CV with his work overseas in Romania, Bulgaria and Poland and is well thought of as a coach and manager.

But a lack of Championship experience is a concern and he is thrown in at the deep end with his new team hovering just above the relegation zone and confidence and morale low.

Iordanescu needs to hit the ground running in a critical week with the Hornets up against mid-table Blackburn and then Derby who are in the relegation zone.

Jensen Huang Says Nvidia's Next-Gen Vera Rubin Chips Will Generate $40 Billion in Revenue per Gigawatt, Up From $25 Billion for Grace Blackwell. What That Means for NVDA's Growth Runway.

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nvidia isn't just achieving higher revenue per gigawatt through increased GPU selling prices.

By Keith Noonan – Nov 25, 2026 at 6:15AM EST

It's been an incredibly wild month for Nvidia (NVDA -5.86%) shareholders. The resignation of CEO Jensen Huang and related dynamics kicked off a surge in selling action that saw the company's share price retreat 48% from the all-time high reached in early October. On Nov. 23, news that Huang had been reinstated as the CEO caused the company's share price to skyrocket 31.5% in a single trading session. The gains added nearly $1 trillion to the company's market capitalization, marking the largest single-day valuation gain for a company in history and spurring a wave of buying across the stock market that saw U.S. stocks rise by roughly $3 trillion.

While Huang's return to Nvidia and the controversies over the smuggling of the company's export-banned chips to China will continue to be heavily discussed and debated in the near term, investors' focus will likely shift back to the company's actual business performance. Along those lines, Huang's estimates for the jump in monetization from the company's next-generation Vera Rubin processing platform look poised to play a powerful role in driving growth.

After generating roughly $25 billion in revenue per gigawatt of compute with its Grace Blackwell platform, Huang sees the Vera Rubin platform generating $40 billion in sales per gigawatt -- good for a 60% increase. In addition to securing more revenue per gigawatt, Nvidia and its shareholders can also bet on a meaningful increase in the number of gigawatts of artificial intelligence (AI) compute as the data center build-out continues at a rapid pace.

According to an estimate from Morgan Stanley, a Vera Rubin VR200 rack will set customers back roughly $7.8 million -- up from roughly $3.99 million for the GB300 rack. Other estimates suggest a lower price increase for the more capable, more energy-efficient racks, but more advanced processors and higher memory content are clearly set to facilitate substantial pricing and sales increases.

On the other hand, more advanced graphics processing units (GPUs) and a greater number of high-bandwidth memory (HBM) chips aren't the only factors behind Nvidia's Vera Rubin platform delivering a big jump in sales per gigawatt compared to Blackwell. The company is also achieving the growth through its related Vera central processing units (CPUs) and new, internally developed networking hardware in its next-generation racks. Nvidia is seeing rapid growth in the CPU space and strong demand for Vera, and it expects that its total revenue from CPUs will more than double next year.

The overall Vera Rubin platform provides a leap that bolsters Nvidia's edge in the AI compute space. In addition to simply generating more revenue per gigawatt, the performance jump provided by its newest GPUs and dramatic market share gains in the CPU category look poised to further strengthen the company's competitive position in the AI infrastructure market.

How AI Has Changed The Build-Versus-Buy Math For Mid-Market Companies

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Mario Žderić is CTO at DECODE, a software engineering company building custom software for mid-market companies and global enterprises.

​If you run a mid-market company, odds are your business depends on off-the-shelf tools that barely fit your processes, with manual work covering the rest. Custom software was always the obvious fix, but building it came with an enterprise price tag.​

The economics of custom software development have changed. For a mid-market budget, you can now get a more complete system, built around how your business actually works and delivered much faster.​

AI-native engineering made it possible. Based on my experience as the CTO of a firm that develops custom software for companies of all sizes, the harder question now is what’s worth building and what isn’t.

Finding people with the skills and expertise to solve complex engineering problems without breaking the bank was the bottleneck for mid-market companies for decades.

AI changed the math: With AI-native workflows, senior engineers can now deliver quality software on a much shorter timeline.​ According to the 2026 Stack Overflow Developer Survey, 83% of developers use at least one AI tool in their work. This is an industry-wide shift.

Integrations, data migrations, tests, documentation and boilerplate used to take up months of work on any project. Now, these tasks get done much faster. On the first fully AI-native project I supervised, an AI concierge app for a European hotel group, the team delivered a scope we initially estimated at 10 months in four. That means we were 2.5x faster than originally estimated.

Our setup made this possible: AI worked from our coding standards, each change went through automated review and QA, and a senior engineer owned every feature from start to finish.​

AI doesn’t change what matters most in development: deciding what to build and understanding the business. That’s still on you.​ However, with a strong in-house engineering team or an experienced external partner, projects that once required an enterprise-level budget are now much more realistic.

The opportunity for mid-market companies isn’t rebuilding every system from scratch. Replacing business-critical core systems like ERPs, accounting and payroll software is rarely worth the risk. The real gain is improving the systems you already rely on without disrupting your team’s daily work.​

For instance, you can build custom software to remove friction slowing down your business: automating workflows between different systems, retiring the 10-year-old spreadsheet holding the business together, removing manual re-entry and replacing brittle no-code automations. That’s where bad data, numbers that don’t match between systems and reports you can’t fully trust come from.​

A U.S. wealth management firm we work with had its work spread across more than a dozen tools and calculated P&L by hand in spreadsheets. No off-the-shelf product fit how it worked, and for a long time, custom solutions were too expensive. Its teams filled the gaps with manual work. Those tools are now one custom platform, built on a mid-market budget.

There’s a second payoff: better AI readiness. According to the RSM Middle Market AI Survey 2026, data quality issues (53%) and integration challenges (47%) are the top barriers among organizations reporting moderate or limited AI pilot success. Custom software can solve both. The same project that makes your operations run more smoothly also provides AI with high-quality, connected data.

The risk with AI is that code is now easier to produce and harder to maintain. Anything you build without engineering discipline will more than likely fail, and your team won’t have the context to fix it.

So, you still need engineers who understand what they’re building.

In the same Stack Overflow survey I mentioned before, only 16% of developers said AI handles complex tasks “very well.” With how fast AI can generate code, there’s more output to review and less time to build context. Engineers split across several projects can’t keep up. ​

That’s why you need a team that only works on your project. They need to fully own and understand the project and your business, whether it’s your own team or an external partner. This lets them challenge bad decisions and catch when an AI agent goes wrong.

Before you do anything, ask these questions:

In my experience, the best place to start is picking two or three high-impact workflows and measuring their costs. Find a monthly figure in hours, errors or delays, whichever applies to the specific workflow.

For business-critical core processes, I typically recommend sticking to off-the-shelf software at first. I wouldn’t recommend going custom unless you’ve outgrown it or need it to solve a unique problem, and your data is clean enough to migrate.

In my view, this is the most important question. Long-term stability and maintainability should be your main goals, so make sure the project has a clear owner and stable team.

If you’re working with external vendors, ask them how many projects their engineers are on and who reviews the code AI writes.

If you can’t answer these questions, you’re not ready.Building custom software is no longer out of reach for most mid-market companies. They have a real opportunity to stop working around software that never fit their needs. The main thing standing in the way is knowing where to start.​​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

Learning things about your partner by spending time with them? Say it ain't so.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

My real relationship be damned, this last week has been all about playing cupid in Grand Theft Auto 6. I've become utterly invested in keeping Jason and Lucia in love, which means a lot of my time in the game has been spent doing things with my partner in tow. Hitting the gym, inviting them out for a late-night bite, robbing a few jewellery stores. You know, couple things. Things couples do.

And, as people tend to do when they're spending a lot of time together, Jason and Lucia talk. A lot. And you know what? Some of the best nuggets of information about their lives—both their time spent together before the events of the game, and their own individual lives before meeting one another—are tucked away in these interactions that fill the game's quieter moments.

Sometimes the interactions are simply fluffy, or funny. Lucia poking fun at Jason for his deep voice and monosyllabic responses, or them reminiscing about little moments that happened in their relationship before the whole "Lucia ghosting Jason and ending up in jail" thing. Sometimes it's Lucia getting a spam call and giving the bot (and eventual human) on the other end of the phone an absolute earful.

But then, occasionally, beyond all the comedic conversations and flavour dialogue that give these characters so much life, the game'll drop some proper good lore. I'll do my best not to spoil stuff here, but for almost every complaint I've seen on social media about why certain protagonists did certain things, there's an answer tucked away in these interactions. The kind of things you'd really want to know about these characters? Your best chance of finding it all out is when you bring them together.

I've really enjoyed having lore drops unfold in mundane conversations while walking around, driving, or eating a burger, rather than hamfisted into the main story. They feel like natural breathing moments for the couple to recount the past, or get into the stuff that was happening in the time that they were apart. You know, the exact kind of times actual couples and friends would find the time to dig into these things in real life, too.

That doesn't mean you have to keep them together all the time. Some of their exclusive missions also provide a great bit of insight into their backstories—like Jason's side stuff with ex-army pal Bret Vogel, or the missions that have Lucia in some all-too-real situations with her mother.

I've played plenty of the game flying solo as both protagonists too, and you'll occasionally get some of this nattering just by hanging around in your abode together. But I can't recommend enough that you find plenty of opportunities to bring them together out in the open world. Even if it's just for 15 minutes here and there, you'll uncover a whole lot about who they were before we first got to know them as the player. And as someone who has really enjoyed learning just about everything about these two as I can, it's been one of my favourite parts of the game so far.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

This month’s Exchange Online and Microsoft Outlook outage has prompted the software giant to declare an unusual “Code Red” internally, Reuters reports. The nearly 24-hour outage took down email for millions of customers, and was triggered by a bug that had been sitting on Microsoft 365 networking gateway servers since July without any impact to servers.

In a memo to the teams responsible for Exchange Online, Microsoft 365 Core president Jason Henderson revealed the outage was the most significant in history for Microsoft’s customers. “Unfortunately, all the work we have done over the years was not sufficient to protect us on Thursday,” said Henderson. “Code Red means that this is the highest priority work for our team.”

As part of this Code Red declaration, Microsoft is focused on load shedding, geographic traffic partitioning, and faster recovery mechanisms. Microsoft now has a target of resolving future outages within an hour, and will reportedly deploy AI agents to help engineers diagnose issues. Microsoft is now holding daily meetings with the engineering group responsible for Microsoft 365 services, alongside weekly reviews.

In a separate memo, Amanda Silver, Microsoft’s corporate vice president of M365 Core and Work IQ, said engineers had to work through a “fog of war” to help resolve the outage. I reported last week that Microsoft engineers had a running bridge call during the entire outage, and at one point it hit the maximum of 1,000 participants.

Microsoft engineers had to exchange messages across 11 Teams chats and reportedly “struggled ​to maintain a cohesive picture” during the outage because of the fragmented internal tracking systems. Sources tell me the outage was marked as “sev0,” the highest level, which indicates a catastrophic level of service disruption. Engineers struggled to get servers back online because they had entered maintenance mode and became unresponsive due to unusually high CPU loads.

Bitcoin ETFs near $5B in record monthly outflows amid AI stock rout

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

US spot Bitcoin ETFs have recorded $4.94 billion in November net outflows, exceeding the previous monthly record of $4.51 billion set in June.

US-listed spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds (ETFs) are approaching $5 billion in monthly net outflows for the first time since their launch in January 2024.

The funds recorded $231.6 million in net outflows on Tuesday, extending their withdrawal streak to six consecutive trading days, according to SoSoValue. November outflows reached $4.94 billion, surpassing the previous monthly record of $4.51 billion in June.

The latest withdrawals bring net outflows for 2026 to about $4.57 billion, erasing the recovery that saw the funds attract $6.34 billion in the third quarter.

Digital asset investment company Teroxx identified rising interest rates and weakness in AI stocks among the factors behind declining ETF demand in its November research report, noting that markets increasingly expect another Federal Reserve rate hike in December.

US spot Bitcoin ETF net flows in billions of dollars, with November data through Nov. 20. Source: Teroxx

“In the near term, flows into Bitcoin will remain under pressure as long as artificial intelligence stocks are searching for a bottom and the December Fed decision looms,” Teroxx digital asset researcher Leonardo Larieira said in comments shared with Cointelegraph.

Larieira said a sustained recovery in Bitcoin ETF inflows would require long-term US Treasury yields to stabilize. “Once that happens, returning institutional capital will meet a regulated infrastructure that is far deeper than it was in August,” he added.

"We sincerely apologize for the gravity of this announcement"

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Capcom has announced that its dinosaur shooter Exoprimal will be unplayable next September as the servers are being taken offline.

While not quite as massive as the year it had in 2026, Capcom's 2023 was incredible, with Resident Evil 4 and Street Fighter 6 releasing as two certified game of the year contenders and as two of the all-time greats in their respective genres. But there was another, namely Exoprimal, a PvPvE shooter where you took a ton of Overwatch-like character classes into brawls where you'd fend off armies of dinosaurs.

It had a bit of a cult following, but Capcom has announced it'll be joining the pile of dead live service titles next year. In a post on the Exoprimal website, Capcom confirms that services for the game will go offline on September 29, 2027, with the post reading, "We cannot thank you enough for the love and support you have shown over the years, and we sincerely apologize for the gravity of this announcement."

As it begins the shutdown process, the game's survival pass premium tiers (aka, the paid battle pass) have been removed from the in-game store already. The game itself will be taken off sale on March 30, 2027, and those who own it will have six more months to play it before it's gone forever. With no way to access the content in single-player, it'll sadly join the ever-growing list of games that are just gone, which certainly isn't a bad omen for the all-digital future.

Exoprimal was perhaps best known for being the bane of many a Dino Crisis fan upon its announcement – especially when there's a character who looks like that game's heroine Regina, who featured heavily in marketing. It was genuinely a fun time when you got into it, but it suffered from the same fate many live services do, that being there's too damn many of them, so it never really took off. It also didn't help that Exoprimal's best content was hidden behind a ton of playtime.

Dino Crisis is more than "Resident Evil with dinosaurs" – it shaped survival horror as we know it.

The S&P 500 Entered Correction Territory. Here’s What History Says Happens Next.

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

By Adria Cimino – Nov 25, 2026 at 6:10AM EST

10 stocks we like better than S&P 500 Index ›

The S&P 500 has soared in recent years on optimism about artificial intelligence (AI) stocks -- investors rushed to get in on these potentially game-changing players, and that movement boosted the overall index. Today, however, AI stocks are doing just the opposite, leading not only the S&P 500 but also the Dow Jones Industrial Average and the Nasdaq Composite lower -- all three indexes finished last week in correction territory. A correction happens when an index falls at least 10% from its most recent peak.

AI stocks have come under pressure amid concerns about the infrastructure build-out. And last week's massive initial public offering of Anthropic -- the first pure-play AI company to go public -- though successful, added to the pressure.

So now, many investors may be asking themselves what's next for the stock market. With the S&P 500 in correction territory, it's a great idea to consider what history has to say.

First, let's take a closer look at the index's path in recent times. After gaining 78% over the past three calendar years, the S&P 500 continued to march higher this year, reaching new record highs. At the same time, pressures mounted, led by worries about tech spending on the AI build-out. Tech giants pledged to spend more than $700 billion this year alone, and investors questioned whether the revenue opportunity would justify such spending.

The Democratic Party's victory in the U.S. midterm elections added a new dimension to this, with the idea that Democrats' policies might lead to AI regulation and a slowdown in development.

Finally, turmoil at Nvidia, the AI industry bellwether, and the Anthropic IPO added further pressure. Nvidia chief Jensen Huang stepped down following allegations of affairs, leaving investors to question how a new leader would define Nvidia's future. (Nvidia's board reinstated Huang 11 days later, and the stock climbed more than 30% in one trading session.)

As for Anthropic, the IPO, the biggest on record, was successful. But it created a liquidity drain as investors sold other stocks in order to buy Anthropic shares. Meanwhile, this also represented an influx of shares of yet another AI giant in an already jittery market.

As a result, indexes declined last week, led by AI stocks. Now, let's consider what history says about what could happen next. I took a look three past S&P 500 market corrections, occurring in March 2022, November 2023, and March 2025. The 2022 correction was followed by a rebound, but over the next six months the index resumed its decline.

In 2023 and 2025, however, the S&P 500 went on to rebound and quickly resume its path higher over the six months to follow. In that period, the index rose 15% and more than 8%, respectively. So, recent history shows us that a correction could be brief and more gains might lie ahead.

It's important to keep in mind, though, that in those past two corrections, the AI boom was in its early stages, and this helped the positive momentum to return. Today, AI is still in its early days, with the actual use of AI just beginning. But AI spending has increased dramatically since just a few years ago. We can see this through the capital spending levels of tech giants.

AMZN Capital Expenditures (TTM) data by YCharts

And since this issue has worried investors, it could continue to represent a headwind at least until we see more evidence that this investment is truly paying off. This could be ongoing demand for chips and a narrowing of the gap between spending and revenue among tech big spenders.

All of this means that while history offers us some clues about what might lie ahead, it's also important to consider the elements driving market movement at the current moment. But here's the good news: Even if this time around the S&P 500 continues its decline or remains in the doldrums, the major indexes have demonstrated that they always go on to recover and win over a period of years. And that's fantastic news for long-term investors.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 796%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 200% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

Delivering "a more modern Garry's Mod" is the goal of spiritual successor S&box's revamped sandbox mode, as classic GMod's workshop addon system returns

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

"I thought that spawning from the cloud was better...but I was wrong"

Facepunch Studios continue to work on S&box, the mix of open source game development platform and Garry's Mod spiritual successor they released to mixed reviews earlier this year. That rough start has it in need of a redemption arc, which the Rust devs are hoping will be powered by going free-to-play and making changes like the revamp of sandbox mode they've just rolled out.

"This new system isn't really new," Newman wrote. "It's the exact same system that Garry's Mod uses. I hold my hands up here, I thought that spawning from the cloud was better...but I was wrong. There definitely is something a lot nicer about installing a big model pack and then having everything right there. I'm sorry it took me so long to come around to realising this."

Similarly, in terms of the updated sandbox mode, Newman noted he feels the team have been "walking the line of making a more modern Garry's Mod really well". "I'm excited by all the possibilities of it," he continued. "Obviously there aren't a ton of addons right now but what we do have is showing a LOT of potential, and that's only going to grow. I've been going through improving things that feel outdated or stupid. I'm resisting bending it all too far, I want it to stay kind of familiar for everyone, but I think we can improve a few things in our new engine."

A number of old school sandbox props, such as chunks of robotics, wheels, gears, and hunks of steel girder are on their way back, while Facepunch are finally ready to add in water. "It has waves," Newman wrote of the latter. "The physics objects get tossed around in the waves. You can go underwater, and swim, and there's caustics and fog and particles and post process effects and stuff." Also, NPCs now "live directly in the engine" making it easier to set up a fight club.

A simplified menu and a whole bunch of performance and dev-focused tweaks round out this update, so go through the full notes if you're keen to read about the likes of workshop mounts and VRAM fragmentation.

As of writing, S&box is still yet to go free-to-play, a change Newman and co previously said is coming "towards the end of the year". If it can nail recapturing the classic GMod magic with updates that players actually want, I think it stands a chance of a comeback. However, as its lifespan thus far has proven, it's harder to rely on high quality user-made games within a game than ever before in an age when people can just resort to using generative AI for an ever-increasing number of things.

‘Shibarium Is Ready Either Way’: SHIB Developer Teases Privacy Boost for Shiba Inu

📅 2026-11-25  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

The Shiba Inu ecosystem may be set for a privacy boost, as longtime SHIB community member and developer Mazrael hints at privacy potential coming to the blockchain.

Shiba Inu's privacy potential draws from Oxbow's Privacy Pools, the privacy design coauthored by Ethereum cofounder Vitalik Buterin, which is currently a plug-in in the Ethereum Foundation's Kohaku wallet SDK.Mazrael noted that Shibarium is now ready for Oxbow's Privacy Pools, with the groundwork already being done.

WOOF 🐶🪖🫡 And it's privacy done the regulation-friendly way. Privacy Pools come from a 2023 paper Vitalik co-wrote: deposits are screened, so stolen funds can't hide in the crowd, while honest users stay private. No KYC, no backdoors, and you can always exit publicly. Two ways to bring it to Shibarium: 0xbow deploys it themselves, or the community deploys the same contracts and runs a relayer. Shibarium is ready either way, and ShibWallet already runs it on testnet 🔥

The groundwork done on Shibarium (Chain ID 109) includes Cancun opcodes, BN254 pairing support for zero-knowledge proofs, CreateX and Arachnid factories and verified PoseidonT3 and PoseidonT4 contracts at Ethereum addresses.

Mazrael shared a GitHub document, "Shibarium (chain 109) support: chain is prepared," which highlighted this fact. Addressed to the Oxbow team was a request: "Hi 0xbow team! We build on Shibarium (chain ID 109) and would love to see Privacy Pools there. We've done the groundwork so a deployment would be as easy as possible, and ShibWallet plans to integrate it through Kohaku from day one."

Mazrael explained that Privacy Pools originated from a 2023 paper Ethereum cofounder Vitalik coauthored: deposits are screened, so stolen funds can't hide in the crowd, while honest users stay private. No KYC, no backdoors and users can always exit publicly.

Two ways to bring it to Shibarium include 0xbow deploys it themselves, or the community deploys the same contracts and runs a relayer.

For the first option, Mazrael has relayed a GitHub request to the Oxbow team, which is currently showing signs of a green light, with Mazrael remarking that "they did the approval thing when we posted basically."

A 0xbow privacy pool deployment for Shiba Inu will bring 'entry point', 'verifiers', and 'PrivacyPoolSimple' for native BONE (Shibarium's gas token). It will also bring 'PrivacyPoolComplex' for SHIB and other ecosystem tokens later.

The Shib ecosystem would become privacy-complian and ShibWallet would integrate it through Kohaku from day one.

Bitcoin price may fall below $53K if bear-market history repeats

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Bitcoin price downside targets focused on the $53,200 realized price as research saw pointed to bear-market patterns continuing for BTC/USD.

Bitcoin (BTC) is approaching investors’ aggregate cost basis, with previous bear markets pointing to the risk of further price declines.

New research from onchain analytics platform Glassnode shows BTC/USD now trades just 10% above its realized price.

Realized price represents the average price at which each Bitcoin last moved onchain. When the spot price falls below this level, currently around $53,200, holders are collectively sitting on an unrealized loss.

BTC/USD one-week chart with realized price. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

During prior bear markets, Bitcoin fell through its realized price before establishing its next long-term floor.

“In each of those bear markets, however, price ended up well below the Realized Price,” Glassnode wrote in the latest edition of its The Week Onchain newsletter.

During Bitcoin’s last bear market in 2022, price spent 180 days below realized price before reclaiming it in January 2023. The record for time spent below investors’ aggregate cost basis is 301 days across 2014 and 2015, per calculations from Bitfinex Alpha, the research arm of crypto exchange Bitfinex.

Applying previous declines below realized price to today’s level would put Bitcoin at about $45,000 in a repeat of the March 2020 crash, or around $41,000 and $37,000 using the 2022 and 2018 bear-market lows, respectively.

“Should this time not be different after all, we would expect price to go lower and close below the Realized Price, which is a little over 10% away,” Glassnode added.

Bitcoin realized price comparison. Source: Glassnode

Both older and younger investor cohorts continue to realize losses onchain following Bitcoin’s fall Saturday to its lowest level since September 2024.

Related: Bitcoin slides amid US tariff nerves as Anthropic falls below IPO price

Glassnode noted that relative to capital inflows, aggregate onchain losses now run at their highest level since the end of the 2022 bear market during the implosion of crypto exchange FTX — a sign that “capitulation has begun.”

“In the past, spikes like this often came close to the lows, as in early 2019, March 2020 and November 2022. They can also come early, as in February 2018 and in May and June 2022, with lower prices still ahead,” it continued.

Bitcoin onchain loss data. Source: Glassnode
