📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

BTC price faces another potential sell-off as it awaits today’s inflation report after weeks of heavy selling. Core PCE is expected to rise 0.3% month over month, while 21Shares puts Bitcoin’s realized price at $53,250. A hotter reading could bring that level closer than we want.

The Binance hack initially sent Bitcoin lower, but the price recovered most of its losses by November 4. The sell-off picked up again afterward, driven by ETF withdrawals, shrinking stablecoin balances, and traders being forced out of leveraged positions.

Core PCE is forecast to rise 0.3% month over month, with annual inflation expected at 3.2%. The figure matters as markets reassess the outlook for interest rates and how much room the Federal Reserve has to ease policy.

🇺🇸8:30 AM ET COULD GET EXPENSIVE FOR OVERLEVERAGED TRADERS.September PCE: 3.6%October forecast: 3.8%Core PCE: 3.1% → 3.2% expected.The Fed wants 2%, but inflation is still sitting significantly above that target.If today’s numbers surprise lower, risk assets could… pic.twitter.com/TNuSlaiwx6

Bitcoin has already suffered a sharp reversal this month. The latest drop followed a brief Nvidia-tied recovery that brought BTC back above $60,000, but momentum quickly faded. A hotter inflation reading could put that recovery further out of reach, as Bitcoin is currently struggling to attract sustained buying.

A softer report could help prices recover, but markets are looking for more than a single green candle after several failed attempts to reverse the decline.

Bitcoin Hyper is down to its final day before launch, and the presale price is still low at just $0.0136. That’s the price buyers can get today, with tomorrow’s launch bringing the presale to a close and exchange trading expected to follow.

More than $33.2 million has already gone into the project. That’s a lot of money for a presale, especially with the deadline now just hours away. Buyers also have 35% APY staking rewards on offer, adding another reason some investors aren’t waiting around for the token to hit exchanges.

The timing is what makes this interesting. Tomorrow, the presale price disappears. Anyone planning to buy after launch will have to enter through the market, where the price could move either way. If demand carries over from the presale, waiting could cost buyers a cheaper entry.

$0.0136 is the presale price today, not tomorrow. With $33.2 million raised and launch just around the corner, anyone still considering HYPER is running out of time.

21Shares puts Bitcoin’s realized price at $53,250, based on Glassnode data from November 22. The figure tracks the average value of Bitcoin based on the price at which coins last moved on-chain.

Bitcoin falling toward that level would put more holders underwater. The firm also outlined a deeper scenario near $43,800, a potential 18% drop below realized price. It considers that outcome less likely, given the institutional presence and spot Bitcoin ETFs that were missing during earlier bear markets.

The report also points to fading demand and forced liquidations as major contributors to BTC price decline. The Binance hack may have triggered the initial selloff, but 21Shares argues that the subsequent weakness was driven by deteriorating market demand.

Bitcoin now needs to reclaim $60,000 and hold it. Otherwise, $53,250 could become the next level as the market searches for a floor.

This Thanksgiving, let’s just not talk about AI

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Maybe we just don’t talk about AI this Thanksgiving.

Of course, we need some common conversation topics to throw around the dining room with friends and relatives we haven’t seen in a while. And though AI seems to be showing up everywhere now, we still have a lot of other stuff to talk about—sports, food, movies, and the like. Or there’s always that thing where you go around the table and everybody says something they’re thankful for. Unless somebody actually says “Claude” (which is fine).

Mostly there are good reasons to avoid the subject of AI at your gathering this year.

  • Too political. Talking politics in mixed company at the Thanksgiving table can have unpredictable, sometimes explosive results. AI has become very political, mainly because of AI data centers. A surprising number of midterm voters cited an aversion to new AI data centers as the main rationale for their swing toward Democrats. Studies have shown that data centers do indeed contribute to rising energy costs, not to mention the noise pollution they produce. In fact, data centers are already so prevalent that there’s a decent chance one of your Thanksgiving guests lives near one. (You may recognize them by the way they keep saying, “Huh? What? Huh?”) Sore subject.
  • Too technical. The last major tech wave, mobile computing, was pretty easy to conceptualize. They turned cellphones into internet devices with big screens and made computer programs called apps to run on them. AI is different. Very few laypeople can explain how a neural network is trained and how it operates. Even PhDs don’t know exactly how AI models develop new skills, some of which have come as a complete surprise. That creates a problem for Thanksgiving conversation. Somebody at the table who listens to a lot of podcasts may seize the moment to nerd-splain AI to everybody. Or worse, they may go on and on about how LLMs are just stochastic parrots—word-guessing machines that will never be capable of anything more. Pedantry, jargon, and boredom will waft through the air around the table. With luck, the speaker will take the blank stares looking back at him as a signal to put a turkey leg in his mouth.
  • Too painful. Somebody at the table might own a bunch of Nvidia or SpaceX stock and get depressed. Or somebody might remind everybody that their retirement accounts are already bound up in risky AI-dependent stocks like SpaceX. Someone may gaze up from their stuffing with a faraway look and recall those simpler times when Jensen Huang was a one-woman man and everybody believed that all the AI companies buying up Nvidia GPUs would actually be able to pay for them, with nary a revenue stream to match. Heady days! They are over. Nvidia has lost 20% of its value this year because maybe bankrolling your profit-free customers the cash to buy your product isn’t the most sustainable business plan. So just don’t bring it up; heavy drinking may ensue.
  • Too personal. Don’t ask people what they use AI for. It can lead to some bad places. Just last year, a 22-year-old sitting across the table from me began talking about all the personal stuff she discussed with ChatGPT. I quickly tuned her out, of course, but there was something in there about an antifungal cream, a GLP-1 “crisis,” and a neurodivergent ex-boyfriend.
  • Too anxiety-inducing. Somebody in the house Thursday may have that soon-to-be-unemployed vibe about them, and it’s quite possible that an AI has already learned to do their job. Challenger, Gray & Christmas found that AI was the top-stated reason for U.S. job cuts through October. Soon that person will be spending their days in the uncanny valley, doing video interviews with wooden-faced AI avatars, only to be passed over (and over) by a candidate-screening algorithm that was offended by their Zoom background.

On a more serious, and more anxious, note, whether people realize it or not, our relationship with AI is pretty different from what it was at this time last year. The models aren’t perfect, or even trustworthy in some applications, but they’ve clearly become more intelligent.

We hear about teams of AI agents working tirelessly on big problems, coding agents building entire apps, and models solving some of the world’s hardest math problems. We may be headed for a world where we coexist with coequal, nonbiological intelligences that are generally as smart as humans. Discussing where this is all headed can make for an uncomfortable conversation, and not one suitable for a holiday gathering. I’d leave it be this Thanksgiving. Turkey legs and existential angst just aren’t a good mix.

The trouble is, AI has seeped into everything, raising the constant question: How is AI affecting this? So you must listen closely for signs of conversational drift toward the AI thing and have some safe, go-to topics ready to throw in. Like these:

  • Remember the World Cup this summer? Wasn’t that nice? For the first time in a while, I felt a tinge of real national pride as I watched the crowds of foreign football fans having a ball alongside Americans in our cities and stadiums. Americans got rave reviews for their hospitality—everybody from Uber drivers to hotel staff to strangers who helped our international guests get around. My favorites were those videos of Europeans discovering the simple joy of chicken tenders, pizza, and french fries slathered in ranch dressing.
  • Returning to the moon. In April, NASA’s Artemis II mission sent a crew of astronauts on a loop around the moon, taking them 252,756 miles from Earth—farther than any humans had ever traveled. They spotted a crater on the lunar surface and named it after the crew commander’s late wife, Carroll.
  • The return of the jaguar. A jaguar nicknamed “Cinco” is roaming southern Arizona’s mountains, only the fifth jaguar seen there since monitoring began 15 years ago. Researchers say it’s the latest sign that the endangered species may be gaining a foothold in the region. They also say that Cinco has a distinctive mark on its face that resembles Groucho Marx’s mustache.
  • Beating cancer. For the first time, 70% of Americans diagnosed with cancer survive at least five years, the American Cancer Society says. In the mid-1970s, only half did.
  • Fewer overdose deaths. U.S. overdose deaths fell about 14% in 2025, the third straight annual drop and the longest decline in decades, according to provisional data from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention.

And finally, Costco’s hot dog and soda combo still costs $1.50, the same as it did in 1985. You can thank me later. Happy Thanksgiving.

Officials from the FBI, CIA and the Office of the Director of National Intelligence briefed staff members of the House and Senate intelligence committees on some of what they know about Alice Huang, Megaspeed, and their links to China’s Ministry of State Security, according to the NYT:

Silicon Valley divorce lawyers are seeing a surge in outreach from CEOs, founders, and high-net-worth employees who are citing the Nvidia scandal as the reason for their calls, according to Fortune’s Emily Forlini.

The revelation of Jensen Huang’s affairs is a “precautionary tale for other CEOs,” said one lawyer who has been involved in the high-profile divorces of some of the tech industry’s most famous CEOs.

Jensen and Lori Huang have been married for 41 years, and reportedly do not have a prenuptial agreement. Jensen Huang is one of the richest people in the world, with a net worth of roughly $137 billion. If Lori leaves, a huge chunk of Jensen’s net worth would potentially transfer to her.

Fed chairman Kevin Warsh will almost certainly add another 0.5% to U.S. interest rates in December, according to the CME FedWatch futures index, and Wall Street is angsty that the move will cause a recession. “The Fed will put the U.S. and [the] world in recession if it continues to raise rates in a failed attempt to drive inflation to 2%,” Bill Ackman said recently.

“The economy is super strong right now, but the Fed is about to kill it,” Jamie Cox of the Harris Financial Group ($1.5 billion AUM) told Fortune.

The problem is that inflation—which at 3.7% is high above the Fed’s 2% target—is being driven by the price of oil (which the Fed can do nothing about) and the price of computer chips and tech equipment (which the Fed can also do nothing about).

If the Fed increases interest rates, it may derail areas of the economy that are sensitive to higher borrowing costs while doing nothing about the roaring trade in oil. AI is increasingly dependent on debt funding, and higher rates could kill off that liquidity.

“Tech drove 44% of GDP growth [in the last 12 months], leaving growth vulnerable as capex slows,” according to Wells Fargo’s Ohsung Kwon. “The fear is hikes into a slowdown, and an overly hawkish Fed could potentially drive recession fears.”

Obviously, the situation with Iran is out of the Fed’s control. But as Fortune’s Ellie Pringle noted yesterday, if Warsh continues to “look through” oil-driven inflation then bond investors will start questioning the Fed’s credibility.

“The path of the war is crucial for the Fed's path,” Kwon said in a note.

Investors have continued to dump tech stocks over the past 24 hours on continued worries about the stability of the AI trade and a report that the Trump administration was proposing a new set of trade tariffs on China, Europe, and 14 other countries. S&P 500 futures moved lower ahead of the open in New York.

“The risk-off tone has returned over the last 24 hours,” Jim Reid and his team at Deutsche Bank said this morning.

At UBS, Paul Donovan said: “The concern is the impact on U.S. inflation, and thus U.S. interest rates. Reports suggest U.S. importers will face only modest tariffs on goods from several countries. There is less certainty about goods from China, but U.S. importers became adept at avoiding those tariffs. A 10% tariff on a finished good translates into around a 4% consumer price increase, as tariffs occur early in supply chains.”

The tech sector saw the worst losses. The tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite fell 1.48% yesterday, much farther than the broader market. In fact, the equal-weight S&P 500—a notional index that values each stock equally—rose 0.53%, suggesting buyers in a range of non-tech stocks.

S&P 500 futures were down again this morning, but Nasdaq 100 futures slipped even farther: down 2.39%, another big step down for an index of its size.

The S&P 500 may have flirted with a downward “correction” around the Nvidia drama, but it’s nowhere near an official bear market, defined by a decline of 20% from the peak. In fact, a bear market is unlikely to happen, according to Deutsche Bank’s Henry Allen. The bank’s official forecast is for the S&P 500 to hit a heady 9,000 by the end of next year.

“It’s worth bearing in mind that the outright bear markets of the last 50 years (S&P 500 down 20% or more) have previously occurred when there was at least one of (1) a recession, (2) widespread expectations of a recession, or (3) after a Fed tightening cycle with multiple hikes.”

Note to self: We will officially be in a tightening cycle if the Fed raises interest rates in December, which it is widely expected to do.

“If these 10 states had followed the rest of the country, up to 45,000 more workers would be in residential construction – about 24,000 of them in specialty trades,” he noted.

Fanatics has become a one-stop shop for sports fans looking to rep their favorite teams and athletes, and now generates roughly $14 billion in annual revenue. For cofounder and CEO Michael Rubin, however, the road to building that empire nearly ended before he turned 20.

When Rubin was just 13, he started a business selling ski equipment out of his parents’ basement in Pennsylvania. A year later, he opened his own storefront. He was making money quickly—and even bought a Porsche before he could legally drive.

But around 1988, there was an unusually warm winter, and so his customers weren’t coming. “It didn’t snow that year,” Rubin recalled recently. “End of the year, I had $200,000 of bills, $80,000 of inventory, and I was sitting right in front of bankruptcy.”

To dig himself out, he negotiated his debts down to $35,000, borrowed money from neighbors, and used the proceeds from a new venture in the closeout business to repay his loans. Five years later, at 21, Rubin was generating $150 million in sales buying and selling closeout merchandise.

“I went from death in its eyes, bankrupt, thought I was a complete failure, giving up on entrepreneurship, to five years later, doing $150 million, making $10 to $15 million a year as a kid,” Rubin said. “And so to me, each disaster, if you learn from it, leads to your next success.”

Where Will SpaceX Stock Be in 5 Years?

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The innovative business is growing fast, but its valuation leaves little room for error.

By Neil Patel – Nov 25, 2026 at 6:06AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Space Exploration Technologies ›

In its short history spanning less than six months as a publicly traded company, Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX -5.65%) shares have been as volatile as the rockets it launches. They quickly soared 25% from the initial public offering opening price to the record close on June 16. Since then, they've fallen 34% (as of Nov. 23).

In addition to its space operations and connectivity segment, the business has developed a strong presence in the artificial intelligence (AI) industry. A lot of moving parts mean that SpaceX seems to be in the news all the time. Investors can find it challenging to focus less on the day-to-day updates and more on the big picture.

When looking at SpaceX, it's important to apply a long-term filter. The company, which sports a significant market capitalization of $1.8 trillion, could be very different in the future. Where will this AI stock be in five years?

In the third quarter (ended Sept. 30), SpaceX's total revenue surged 161% year over year to $14.6 billion. Viewed from above, there are three distinct businesses.

The superintelligence segment, the company's AI unit, is the biggest. Its revenue skyrocketed to $7.6 billion from $813 million a year earlier. Most of it comes from renting data center capacity to AI developers like Anthropic and Alphabet's Google Cloud. The Cursor coding tool and the Grok chatbot add to the total.

It won't be cheap to achieve this growth. Capital expenditures totaled $48.1 billion in the first nine months of 2026, with another $35 billion to $40 billion expected in Q4. Operating cash flow doesn't come close to covering this bill. The consensus estimate among sell-side analysts is that free cash flow will be negative $146 billion in 2027.

The five-year question doesn't pertain to revenue. It's mainly about whether all this spending eventually produces cash.

At a $1.8 trillion market cap, SpaceX trades at a price-to-sales (P/S) ratio of 56.3. That's based on trailing-12-month revenue of $32 billion. That looks absurdly expensive. But backward-looking numbers can be misleading when a company changes this fast.

Annualize the $35 billion midpoint of Q1 2027 guidance, and the P/S multiple falls to 12.9. Against Morningstar's 2030 forecast, it's just 7.9. That math is better the further out you look.

For what it's worth, the competitive advantages are real. SpaceX lifted more than 80% of the world's mass to orbit in 2025, thanks to its reusable rockets. That cost edge lets it deploy Starlink satellites more cheaply than anyone else, building a network rivals would struggle to replicate.

However, the economic moat comes from space-related endeavors. The growth is coming from AI, a far less defensible business.

Anthropic, SpaceX's largest AI customer, can exit its agreements with 90 days' notice once their initial terms end in early 2027. Google's deal has a similar exit clause. This is the risk investors are probably overlooking the most.

It makes any five-year outlook highly uncertain. You see this in the revenue forecasts. Add Starship execution risk and heavy cash burn, and the range of outcomes is enormous.

Even with strong execution, SpaceX's steep valuation could leave shareholders with disappointing returns. Investors can't ignore the downside.
